# Judge Dredd Miniatures Game (Warlord Games, 2020) — Rulebook

*Verbatim text extraction, page by page, from the embedded PDF text layer (PyMuPDF `page.get_text("text")` — no OCR needed, clean embedded text confirmed throughout all 164 pages). Page numbers below are PDF page indices (1-164), which run slightly ahead of the book's own printed page numbers because of unnumbered front-matter pages. Extraction is presented as continuous running text per page; the source uses a two-column-ish layout in places so occasional line-order artefacts are possible around images and sidebars — cross-check against `original.pdf` if an exact wording matters for a rules dispute.*

*Layout tables (weapon stat blocks, campaign tables, sentencing tables) mostly extracted in reasonable reading order but are NOT reconstructed as markdown tables here — they read as label/value pairs in sequence. Where a table's exact numbers matter for analysis, they were cross-checked by eye against this extraction; see the vault note for the tables actually used.*

## PDF Page 7

Judge DreDD
INTRODUCTION  
Ok, it’s 1982 and I will almost certainly be late for 
work, but I need to detour to my local newsagents 
to pick up the latest issue of 2000 AD. The 
newsagent looks down his nose at me, I’m clearly 
too old to be reading comics at my age, but he 
accepts my 18p and I walk to the bus stop 
engrossed in this week’s ﬁx of Thrill‐Power, all the 
time narrowly dodging lamp posts and other 
mundane real life obstructions. Now, don’t get me 
wrong, whilst I loved Nemesis, Strontium Dog, Halo 
Jones, Skizz and Rogue Trooper, the ﬁrst story I 
always went to, without fail, was Judge Dredd.  
The venerable Judge ﬁrst appeared in the pages of 
2000 AD back in 1977, a creation of writer John 
Wagner, 2000 AD editor Pat Mills and the artist 
Carlos Ezquerra. A classic anti‐hero, Dredd’s 
character was almost certainly inspired by Clint 
Eastwood’s ‘Dirty Harry Callaghan’ persona but 
taken to the next outrageous level.  
The Dredd stories are set against the backdrop of 
Mega‐City One during the ﬁrst half of the 22nd 
century. The ‘Big Meg’, is a huge, densely 
populated urban sprawl incorporating most of the 
eastern seaboard of the United States of America – 
or rather what was left of it after a devastating 
nuclear war. Mega‐City One is inhabited by 
hundreds of millions of citizens, whose day‐to‐day 
mental state ranges from the mildly disturbed to 
just plain crazy. Policing such a turf clearly requires 
an extraordinary kind of cop – a judge, jury and 
executioner all rolled into one.  
Enter the Judges. Speciﬁcally Judge Dredd, a hard‐
nosed, near indestructible individual given the role 
of enforcer over the Big Meg’s mean streets. Unlike 
many characters in a long running comic series 
Dredd has aged in real‐time, allowing us to see how 
his world has changed. Yet he himself remains as 
inﬂexible and indomitable as ever – over 70 years 
old but still at the height of his power. 
Despite the Dredd saga being set in a grim, 
dystopian world, I believe its most endearing 
aspect is that whilst its namesake character 
appears to have no sense of humour himself, the 
stories are liberally laced with a rich seam of black 
comedy which takes numerous irreverent pokes at 
contemporary society. It’s also quite astonishing 
how many seemingly crazy predictions in old 
Dredd stories have now come to pass in our  
current world. 
The world of Dredd has expanded well beyond its 
original boundaries and is constantly being 
enriched with a dazzling array of complex and 
interesting supporting characters. Often these 
characters have their own compelling stories to 
tell, some even earning their own spin‐oﬀ story 
arcs. Characters like Judges Anderson and Hershey 
have become stalwart allies, gaining the grudging 
respect of ‘Old Stony Face’. Conversely, a multitude 
of bad guys, including the likes of Judge Death and 
Orlok the Assassin, have returned again and again, 
pushing Dredd to the limits but never defeating 
him. It’s no wonder that the world of Dredd 
inspired two ﬁlms, an upcoming TV series and an 
expanding library of novels, audio dramas and, of 
course, games. 
It’s now 2019 and here I am writing an introduction 
to a new Judge Dredd miniatures game.  
Whilst the background of many of Dredd’s most 
famous adventures are truly apocalyptic, including 
the nuclear exchange of the Apocalypse War, the 
usual encounters illustrated in the panels of the 
Dredd strip are day‐to‐day small skirmishes 
between factions ﬁghting for control of Mega‐City 
One’s streets. It is the aim of this game to enable 
players to recreate those classic scenarios on the 
tabletop, using no more than a dozen ﬁgures a 
side. The game builds on the well‐received 
Strontium Dog ruleset penned by Andy Chambers 
and Gav Thorpe and is suitably adapted to the 
world of Judge Dredd. 
Within these pages you will not only ﬁnd game 
stats for the Justice Department and the criminal 
groups that stalk the streets but also the barely 
controlled Citi‐Def militia, groups of crazies and 
many other colourful denizens of Mega‐City One.  
As we add more models, characters and factions in 
future expansions you will have the opportunity to 
recreate some of the greatest storylines that have 
ever graced the pages of 2000 AD and more 
recently the Judge 
Dredd Megazine. 
 
– Roger  Gerrish 
(Somewhere  
in Brit‐Cit)

## PDF Page 8

6
MEGA-CITY ONE 
Mega-City One was founded in 2032 as an 
answer to the intense overcrowding facing 
the United States and it formed the model 
for other such massive complexes across 
the world. Originally envisioned to home 
around 350 million inhabitants, by the turn 
of the 22nd century the population had 
risen to over 800 million. The City covers 
an area from southern Maine all the way 
down to Florida, and to the west stretched 
into Ohio. During the Atomic War of 2070 
the city took a number of debilitating 
strikes, but its strategic defence lasers 
protected it when much of the surrounding 
country was totally devastated. In its 
heyday the city was believed to have at 
least one million miles of roadway in 
constant use and even stretched its 
influence to the stars, founding a number 
of flourishing, if troublesome, extra-solar 
colonies. Yet, since the Apocalypse War 
with East-Meg One in 2104 the population 
has been drastically reduced by a series of 
catastrophic disasters including Sabbat’s 
Judgement Day, the Necropolis event, and 
more recently the Day of Chaos, where a 
combination of plague and anarchy reduced 
the population to roughly 50 million. This 
left the city in ruins with its economy on 
its knees. Despite this, the Big Meg lives 
on. Now, with the relaxation of immigration 
laws, support from its colonies and other 
initiatives, few can doubt that Mega-City 
One will rise again.

## PDF Page 10

NEeDED FOR PLAY 
In addition to this rulebook, you will also need several 
other items to play skirmish games with Judge Dredd 
models. Materials such as terrain pieces are not strictly 
required for the basic game. However, their inclusion 
will enhance your games. You will need: 
• Two or more players (two to four is the  
suggested range); 
• A playing surface, such as a tabletop or 
unobstructed ﬂoor space; 
• A selection of Judge Dredd models; 
• A tape measure or ruler a minimum of 12"  
in length; 
• Assorted markers (Pinned markers, Stunned 
markers, Injury markers, Overwatch markers and 
Moving Fast markers); 
• A variable number of Action chips and Star chips 
(see page 14); 
• Combat dice (at least six); 
• Some six‐sided dice (just one will do). 
OVERVIEW OF THE GAME 
Judge Dredd is a tabletop skirmish battle game for two or 
more players. Players take control a small team of Justice 
Department personnel, Block Gangers, Citi‐Def militia, or 
any number of weird and wonderful factions on the streets 
of Mega‐City One.  
USING THIS BOoK 
This book contains all the rules you need to play Judge 
Dredd, including scenarios and proﬁle lists of the Mega‐City 
One denizens used to create your own gangs and factions. 
HOW TO GET STARTED 
Start by taking a little time to read over 
the next section – the core game 
mechanics – to get a handle on the 
general game rules. By the time you’ve 
done that you’ll know enough to play a 
game. For a ﬁrst outing, ﬁght with just a 
few models per side. As you grow more 
conﬁdent, try some larger games with 
more models.

## PDF Page 11

BASIC GAME MECHANICS 
DICE 
Judge Dredd uses special 
six‐sided Combat dice 
(sometimes individually 
referred to as die) 
for resolving 
combat and 
random events. 
Ordinary six‐sided dice are also sometimes used for 
generating random results in relation to scenarios, 
various charts and tables, and so forth. 
Combat dice 
Combat dice have the following faces: 
[HIT] x3  
[ARMOUR] x2 
[SPECIAL] x1
For a 'D3' result , roll a D6 and halve the result, rounding 
up, as shown on the following table. 
Re-rolls 
Sometimes players will have the ability to re‐roll dice. 
This lets the player pick up dice that have produced an 
unsatisfactory result and roll them again. The result of 
the re‐roll always stands even if it is worse than the 
original. Re‐rolled dice may never be re‐rolled again. 
Dice modiﬁers 
Combat dice are modiﬁed by rolling more or fewer 
Combat dice depending on circumstances. For 
example, a target is at long range, so shooting  
attacks with some weapons will roll one less 
Combat dice to hit. Combat dice results 
themselves are never modiﬁed. 
Modiﬁers can apply to the results 
of ordinary dice rolls. Dice 
modiﬁers can be negative (e.g. 
‐1 to the result) or positive 
(e.g. +1 to the result).
CORE RULES
ORDINARY DICE 
When referencing dice rolls, the rules will refer to 
ordinary six‐sided dice as D6. Occasionally you might be 
called upon to roll two or more D6 and add the results 
together. Where this the case, the roll is referred to as a 
‘2D6’ or ‘3D6’ roll. More commonly, you’ll need to roll a 
D6 and add a number to the result. Where this is the 
case, it is shown as D6+1 or D6+2, etc.

## PDF Page 12

MEASURING  
In Judge Dredd measuring distances for movement and 
shooting is done using the model’s base. 
Movement  
When moving, no part of a model’s base can travel 
beyond the allotted distance. 
Shooting 
When shooting, measure from the closest point on the 
shooting model’s base to the closest point on the target 
model’s base. 
10
MARKERS 
Markers are used in the game to keep track of  
model status and to cut down on bookkeeping.  
These markers are: 
ACTION CHIP – Shows when a 
model has activated. 
STAR CHIP – Like Action chips, 
but are better (you’ll see).  
INJURY MARKER – Placed on 
models to show they are hurt 
and will perform less well. 
Each Injury marker on a model 
is cumulative – accrue enough 
and your model is incapacitated 
and out of the ﬁght. 
PINNED MARKER – Used to show 
that a model is distracted by 
enemy attacks, over‐extended 
or vulnerable in some way.  
STUNNED MARKER –  
A temporary kind of Injury 
marker. Not as nasty as a 
full Injury marker as it is 
easier to recover from.  
MOVING FAST MARKER –  
Used to mark models that 
are Sprinting or Charging. 
OVERWATCH MARKER –  
To mark models carefully 
covering an area with their 
weapon ready to shoot if a target 
TURNING AND FIRE ARCS 
Most models in Judge Dredd may turn freely when 
moving but are limited to only ﬁring at targets to their 
front. However, some larger models have special rules 
limiting them to represent size, speed, and/or poor 
reaction times.  
Prime examples are vehicles. Equally, aliens, mutants, 
or robots may possess capabilities (like eyestalks, 
cameras, or swivelling joints) that permit them to shoot 
or move outside of human norms. 
Turning and ﬁre arcs are covered in more detailed 
later, but the same designations are used throughout 
the rules.  
Regarding models,  
the normal primary 
facings, and ﬁre arcs 
are 180 degrees Front 
(F) and Back (B), Left 
(L) and Right (R). 
Some models may have 
weaponry or turning 
that is limited to 
‘focused’ arcs. These 
are limited to 90 degrees 
and are marked with the extra preﬁx ‘F’ (FF, FR, FL, and 
FB). Such limited ﬁelds of ﬁre are common for long or 
heavy weapons.

## PDF Page 13

11
MEASURING INCREMENTS 
Judge Dredd uses the archaic measuring increment 
known as the ‘inch’, only familiar to older wargamers, 
and the inhabitants of the North American Mega‐Cities 
and Brit‐Cit. 
A measuring tape or ruler marked with one inch 
increments is really useful here, but if you can only get a 
metric measuring device then simply double all the 
distances noted in the rules as being in inches and you’ll 
get to much the same place. 
Distances in inches are shown with a number followed 
by ", so twelve inches = 12". 
The playing area for Judge Dredd is intended to be three 
feet by three feet (36" x 36") – so a medium sized table 
should be ideal for your games. 
Pre-measuring 
Being able to pre‐measure ranges, move distances and 
the like is permitted by default in Judge Dredd. If players 
wish to disallow pre‐measuring by mutual agreement 
that’s perfectly ﬁne of course, but the default for this 
option is ‘ON’. 
MODEL OVERVIEW 
Judge Dredd is played with individual models 
representing each ﬁghter on the battleﬁeld. Most 
models act independently during a ﬁght, but some 
types of models may be grouped together into Packs to 
make them more eﬀective. 
MODEL STATISTICS 
Models have statistic scores (stats) to tell you just how 
good each model is in battle.  
The core stats are: 
Move 
A model’s Move stat tells you how far it can move in a 
single action. The Move of a model might get modiﬁed 
temporarily if moving across diﬃcult ground or 
climbing, for example, or more permanently by being 
injured or stunned. Typical humanoid movement rates 
vary between 3" and 6" depending on encumbrance and 
ﬁtness. Mounted models such as those riding bikes can 
have a move of 7" to 12" or more! 
Shoot 
The Shoot stat gives the number of Combat dice to roll 
when the model makes a ranged attack. This means 
that models with Shoot 6 are exceptionally deadly 
marksmen, while those with Shoot 1 are more likely to 
score hits only through pure luck. 
Fight 
The Fight stat indicates the number of Combat dice 
rolled when the model makes a close combat attack. As 
with Shoot, a model’s Fight stat can vary between 6 for 
a martial arts master and 1 for a clueless sap. 
Evade 
The Evade stat gives the number of Combat dice 
rolled when the model attempts to dodge an 
incoming ranged or close combat attack. Not all 
models have an Evade statistic, but it can be gained 
or enhanced by technology or certain psychic or alien 
powers. Evade can range between 0 and 6. However 
an Evade stat better than 1 or 2 is a rarity amongst 
normal individuals. 
Resist 
A model’s Resist stat measures how hard it is to 
damage the model with an attack. When a model is hit, 
its Resist stat tells you how many Combat dice to roll to 
try and stop the damage. Resist can be enhanced with 
additional armour, cover or special powers.  
Cool 
A model’s Cool is a measure of how well it can keep  
its head (or equivalent) and function eﬀectively in  
a combat situation. In the most direct terms, it affects 
how many actions a model can potentially take  
during a game turn, but it also aﬀects a model’s ability 
to withstand the eﬀects of getting pinned down  
or escaping tricky situations. Cool varies between 5  
for a veteran Judge like Joe Dredd and 1 for a  
panicked civilian. 
Notoriety 
A model’s Notoriety represents its relative power, 
inﬂuence, eﬀectiveness and fame (or infamy). It is used 
when building factions for either a single game or linked 
campaign – see page 141 for more details.
TAKING A STAT TEST 
At various times the rules will call on models to test against 
their stats to achieve things like dodging an enemy attack or 
keeping their cool under ﬁre. That means you roll one 
Combat dice for each point of that stat the model has 
at the time. Unless speciﬁcally stated otherwise, a 
stat test needs at least one [SPECIAL] result to pass it. 
Core Rules

•  Shoot………

## PDF Page 14

Range   
The Range stat determines a weapon’s range in inches 
on the tabletop. Range is divided into two bands, short 
and long. Some close combat weapons (like knives) can 
also be thrown and have a long range to show this, but 
other close combat weapons can’t be thrown so don’t 
have a long range. 
Modiﬁers 
Diﬀerent ranged weapons have modiﬁers to the Shoot 
stat of the model using them to increase or decrease 
the number of Combat dice rolled by a shooting model. 
Diﬀerent modiﬁers apply at long and short range 
according to the weapon types.  
Power 
Power describes how hard‐hitting a weapon is. If a 
shot hits a model, the weapon’s Power is compared to 
the model’s Resist stat to see if it injures them and 
how badly. 
Special Rules 
If a weapon has any special rules that apply to it, for 
example Stun, Blast or Incendiary, they are noted in the 
Special Rules section. 
12

Many models will be armed with one or more ranged 
weapons, usually a gun of some sort. Weapons have 
their own stats just like models do.  
When a model attacks, it uses  
its own stats combined with  
those of the weapon it is carrying 
to calculate the result.  
Ranged weapon stats are: 
• Range 
• Modiﬁer 
• Power 
• Special Rules

## PDF Page 16

DRAWING ACTION CHIPS 
Action chips are drawn from the bag, one at a time,  
and handed to the owning player as they are drawn. 
Normally all actions generated by an Action chip are 
resolved before the next chip is drawn, although some 
special conditions may vary this. 
PLAYING ACTION CHIPS 
When a player gains an Action chip, they choose a 
model to activate and take actions with it.  
A model gets two single actions or a single double‐
action when it activates. Typical actions include: 
Single Action 
• Move 
• Take a Snapshot 
Double Action 
• Sprint 
• Take an Aimed Fire shot 
Choosing which model to activate 
A player can select any one of their models on the 
tabletop that hasn’t yet activated to use an Action chip. 
Leave the Action chip beside the model after it has 
completed its actions to show that it has activated this 
turn and can’t be activated again. 
ACTIVATING WITH A STAR CHIP 
When a Star chip is used to activate a model, there’s a 
chance that the chip isn’t left on the tabletop 
afterwards and is instead returned to the bag. This 
leaves the model free to activate again later in the turn, 
it also means that the Star chip is in the bag ready to 
come out again later in the turn. Star chips are good. 
14
MAYOR DAVE  
Possibly the most popular Mayor of Mega-City One, Dave 
the Orangutan was allowed to run for office simply because 
no law prevented it. This being Mega-City One, after Dave 
was nominated he won by a landslide victory! Judge Dredd 
at the time considered it no bad thing as the ape was a 
“definite improvement" over the previous holders of the 
office. Many years later Dredd cited Dave as the reason 
why democracy could not work - let the people decide, and 
they give you an ape for mayor! 
THE GAME TURN 
ACTION CHIPS 
Judge Dredd is played using a system 
of drawing Action chips to see when 
models can activate during the game turn  
and determining just how much they can achieve.  
Once all models have activated at least once, the  
game turn is over. 
At the start of the game, you’ll need one Action chip for 
each model under your control.  
Each player uses diﬀerent colour Action chips and all  
of the chips for the game are placed together in a bag, 
cup or other opaque container at the start of each  
game turn. 
Example: Wayne has 5 models so gets 5 blue Action 
chips placed in the bag. Karl’s models total only 3 so 
he gets 3 gold Action chips in the bag. 
STAR CHIPS 
For models with a Cool of 4 or 
greater put a special Star Action chip 
in the bag instead of a normal one. 
For example, Wayne has four models in  
the game. Two of Wayne’s models have a Cool of 4 
or more, so two normal Action chips and two Star 
chips in Wayne’s team colour are placed in the bag. 
Note that robots and A.I. controlled vehicles do not 
generate Star chips, even if they are Cool 4 or higher. 
The fact that such models are harder to pin down  
with attacks in no way makes them more suited for 
heroic action. 


---

## PDF Page 17

15
ENDING THE GAME TURN 
A game turn ends when all the models on the tabletop 
have an Action chip beside them indicating they have 
activated this turn (potentially this can be more than 
once using Star chips), or if there are no more Action 
chips left in the bag. 
At the end of the game turn: 
1. Gather up all the Action chips and place them back in 
the bag. Do not replace Action chips or Star chips for 
models that have become casualties and remove any 
excess Action and Star chips from the bag. 
2. Check for any speciﬁc scenario events. 
3. Check victory conditions (see the Scenarios section 
on page 122). 
You are now ready to begin the next game turn. 
ENDING THE GAME  
The game ends depending on the speciﬁc scenario rules 
– see pages 122‐139. 
Going for broke 
Once a model completes its activation from a 
Star chip, the owning player may take a Cool 
test for the model by rolling one Combat dice 
for each point of Cool the model currently has. If any of 
the Combat dice roll a [SPECIAL] result, the Star chip is 
returned to the bag and the model may activate again 
later in the turn with another Action chip (either an 
ordinary one or a Star chip). Players are never obliged 
to attempt a ‘going for broke’ Cool test. 
Theoretically, a model could keep being activated with 
a Star chip and continue to pass its ‘going for broke’ 
Cool test until the heat death of the universe occurs. In 
practice though, passing the test is very unpredictable 
so don’t count on it happening too much, even with 
models that have Cool 5! 
Note that Star chips do not have to be used on models 
with a Cool of 4 or greater when they are drawn. This 
represents the leadership and conﬁdence (or fear) 
instilled by having an experienced or intimidating 
character in the model’s faction. Clearly the chance of a 
model with a lower Cool passing the test to return the 
Star chip to the bag is lower, but it can still happen. 
Failure has its price! 
Failing a ‘going for broke’ Cool test to return a Star 
chip to the bag comes at a price. The Star chip is left 
in place to indicate that the model cannot activate 
again this turn, and it also gains a Pinned marker to 
indicate its overextended and somewhat precarious 
status. For more on Pinned markers see the combat 
rules on page 21. 
Last chip in the bag 
If a Star chip is the last one pulled from the bag, the 
activating model can still attempt to return it for 
another activation. However, a ‐2 Cool modiﬁer applies 
to this and all subsequent Cool tests to return the chip 
to the empty bag. 
MULTI-PLAYER GAMES 
In multi‐player games, each player will need their own easily 
identiﬁable set of Action chips for their models. Beyond this 
requirement, the rules are the same as for a two‐player game 
– when a player’s Action chip is drawn, they can activate one 
of their models. 
Judge Dredd speeds through 
clouds of Stumm Gas.

## PDF Page 18

ACTIONS 
When a model activates it can take either two single 
actions or a double‐action. 
SINGLE ACTIONS 
• Move – Move the model. 
• Snapshot – Shoot a weapon with the model. 
• Throw – Throw a weapon with the model. 
• Fight – Move the Model up to 3" and make a close 
combat attack. 
• Shake it oﬀ! – Remove a Pinned or Stunned 
marker from the model.
DOUBLE ACTIONS 
• Aimed Fire – The model Shoots a weapon with  
a bonus. 
• Sprint – Move the model up to twice its Move  
plus D6". 
• Charge – Move the Model up to its Move plus  
+D6", and make a close combat attack. 
• Set Overwatch – Set the model to cover an area 
ready to shoot. 
• Hunker Down – Attempt to remove an Injury 
marker from the model. 
NOMINATING ACTIONS 
You don’t need to declare both of a model’s actions 
before undertaking them. This means you could, for 
example, take a Snapshot at a target and then decide to 
Snapshot again if you missed it, or Move to get  
into cover instead.

## PDF Page 19

SINGLE ACTIONS  
MOVE 
A commonly used action that’s handy for moving 
models around the tabletop. 
Moving 
A model taking a Move action is moved across the table 
a number of inches equal to its Move stat.  
A model can normally turn as many times as it likes 
during its move, but it must follow a deﬁned path to 
reach its destination. The model may end its Move 
action facing in any direction. 
You can move through friendly models without penalty. 
Terrain eﬀects on movement 
See the Terrain section (page 34) for details on how 
features on the tabletop can aﬀect Move actions. 
SNAPSHOT 
When a model takes a Snapshot, it turns in place and 
uses one of its ranged weapons to shoot at a target.  
A Snapshot represents ﬁring ‘from the hip’ with little or 
no aiming.  
For a more careful aimed shot, a double‐action is 
required (see Aimed Fire on page 18).  
Some weapons are too big or slow to reload to take 
Snapshots with and require an Aimed Fire to shoot at 
all. This is noted in the weapon's Special Rules. 
THROW 
A model using a Throw action makes a ranged attack 
just like a Snapshot. The big diﬀerence in this case is the 
nature of the weapon used. It is resolved using the 
model’s Fight stat instead of its Shoot. Throw can only 
be used with weapons that have the Throw special rule. 
FIGHT 
A Fight action allows a model to move up to 3" (as for a 
Move action) and strike at an enemy model with a close 
combat attack. 
SHAKE IT OFf! 
This action is used when a model is pinned or stunned. 
Its eﬀect is to remove one Pinned or one Stunned 
marker from the model. Breath is being caught, 
weapons are being reloaded, insults and pithy 
comments swapped, and new strategies discussed 
during this brief interlude. 
TROGgIES 
The Troggies are 
descendants of a 
20th century cult 
who could not cope 
with the pressure 
of modern 
technology and so 
retreated underground 
where their bodies 
mutated to adapt to 
the subterranean 
environment. During the 
early 22nd century 
they returned to what 
was now Mega-City 
One and started to 
abduct Mega-City 
citizens to be used as 
slave labourers. Led by Slick Willy, they 
planned to eventually destroy the city  
with carefully placed subterranean 
explosives. Finally defeated by Dredd they 
were all apparently arrested, although 
some may yet wander the vast expanses 
of the Undercity. 
Core Rules
Death lives! Judge Dredd and his Deadworld 
foe tangle in the supernatural mist.

## PDF Page 20

18
DOUBLE-ACTIONS  
SPRINT 
When a model takes a Sprint double‐action, it executes 
two Move actions, one after another, with a bonus of 
+D6" of movement added at any point. A sprinting 
model can only move into its front arc during each 
move. Mark the sprinting model with a Moving Fast 
marker. Moving Fast markers and their eﬀects remain in 
place until the model is activated next.  
CHARGE  
A Charge double‐action works much like a Fight action 
but with an additional Move action. The charge must be 
directed towards an opposing model or vehicle in the 
model’s Front (F) arc. Roll a D6 and add the result to the 
model’s Move. If the charging model can move within 
close combat range (1") of the target, it makes a close 
combat attack with a bonus of +1 to its Power. Mark the 
charging model with a Moving Fast marker. You may 
charge even if already engaged in close combat. 
Moving Fast markers and their eﬀects remain in place 
until the model is next activated.  
Models may attempt to use Charge double‐actions to 
break through doors and windows, see page 37. 
AIMED FIRE 
An Aimed Fire double‐action is a ranged attack 
resolved in the same way as a Snapshot. Turn the 
model in place as desired before taking the shot. 
Aimed Fire may only be made into the model’s 
Focused Front ﬁre arc (FF). In the case of Aimed 
Fire, the attacking model can elect to add +2 
Combat dice to either its Shoot stat (to represent 
more careful aiming) or the weapon’s Power 
(representing aiming for a vulnerable spot) for the 
Aimed Fire ranged attack. 
Violence erupts on 
the streets near 
Leroy Jenkins Block.


---

## PDF Page 21

19
SET OVERWATCH 
Turn the model in place as desired and then place an 
Overwatch marker beside the model. During any 
subsequent activation, the model can make a ranged 
attack against a model that takes an action within the 
overwatching model’s Focused Front ﬁre arc (FF). If the 
target is taking a Move action of any kind (including 
Sprint or Charge), the Overwatch attack is a Snapshot. 
For any other types of action, it is an Aimed Shot. The 
overwatching model can choose when to take the shot 
(waiting for a moving model to come out of cover for 
example), but must have the target within its range, ﬁre 
arc and line of sight at the point when the Overwatch 
attack is made. 
Remove the Overwatch marker from the model when it 
has taken its shot. If an overwatching model is pinned, 
stunned or incapacitated before using the marker, it 
loses its Overwatch marker too. Otherwise, the 
Overwatch marker remains in place – even into 
subsequent game turns – until the overwatching model 
uses it or activates and takes a diﬀerent action. 
HUNKER DOWN  
A Hunker Down double‐action allows an injured model 
a chance to get into cover and recuperate. Roll a D6 and 
add the score to the model’s Move stat (keeping in 
mind this will be reduced by any Injury markers and 
Stunned markers it has). The model can move up to this 
total distance in any direction providing the move ends 
no closer to the nearest enemy model.  
If the model moves into cover, roll a number of Combat 
dice equal to its original, unmodiﬁed Resist 
stat. For each [ARMOUR] result rolled, the model 
removes one Injury marker down to a minimum 
of one (i.e. an Injured model can’t remove the 
last Injury marker from itself by using Hunker Down). 
PACKS 
Whilst some models such as Juves, citizens or Dog 
Vultures may be fairly ineﬀective as individuals 
they can become quite dangerous when they’re 
acting as a collective mob or pack. A pack 
comprises multiple models that are all activated 
together by a single Action chip. Individual pack 
members are usually weak but become more 
dangerous in a group. The following restrictions 
apply to packs: 
• Regardless of the number of models in the 
pack, a pack only ever generates a single 
Action chip. 
 
• All the models in the pack must choose the 
same actions when activated. 
• All of the models in a pack must end their 
activation within 3" of at least one other 
model from the same pack. 
• When the pack attacks in ranged or close 
combat, pick a single model to make the 
attack. Any other models in the pack that can 
join the attack (i.e. if they have the range and 
line of sight to do so) add their Shoot or Fight 
stats to the attacking model for the attack.

## PDF Page 22

20
RANGED ATtACK ACTION SUMmARY 
1.
Pick a ﬁring model and a target model, stating which 
weapon or weapons will be used. 
2. Check range and line of sight to the target model from the 
ﬁring model. 
3.
Take a number of Combat dice equal to the ﬁrer’s Shoot stat. 
4. Add or subtract Combat dice depending on the Range 
Modiﬁer of the weapon being used. 
5.
Roll the Combat dice. If any [HIT] results are rolled, the 
target has been hit.  
6. If the number of [HIT] results rolled equals or exceeds the 
target’s Cool, the target is also pinned. 
7.
The target model can attempt to dodge a hit by rolling a 
number of Combat dice equal to its Evade stat. If any 
[SPECIAL] results are rolled, the hit is negated. 
8. Roll a number of Combat dice equal to the weapon’s Power 
stat. Count up the number of [HIT] results scored. 
9. Roll a number of Combat dice equal to the target  
model’s Resist stat. Count up the number of [ARMOUR] 
results scored. 
10. Deduct the total number of the target’s [ARMOUR] results 
from the total number of the weapon’s [HIT] results. If any 
[HIT] results are left over, the target suﬀers one injury  or 
stun per remaining [HIT]. 
11. Place Injury or Stun markers and lay down, or otherwise 
mark, models that are incapacitated or subdued.
CHECK RANGE AND LINE OF SIGHT 
Find the distance between the ﬁring model and the 
target model by measuring a straight line across the 
shortest distance between the ﬁring model’s base and 
the target’s base. Check this against the Range stat of 
the weapon being used. 
Fire arcs 
Most models have limited ﬁre arcs for their weapons. 
The model must lie inside a weapon’s ﬁre arc for it to  
be claimed as being within line of sight.  
General ﬁre arcs are: 
• One‐handed ranged weapons: Front (F). 
• Two‐handed ranged weapons: Focused Front (FF). 
• Heavy weapons: Focused Front (FF). 
Line of sight 
Draw a straight line across the shortest distance 
between the shooting model’s base and the target 
model’s base. 
If there are terrain or models in the way that are taller 
than both the target model and the shooting model, 
the line of sight is blocked and a diﬀerent target must 
be chosen. 
HITtING A TARGET  
Take a number of Combat dice equal to the ﬁrer’s  
Shoot stat. Apply the appropriate dice modiﬁers for the 
weapon’s Range stat and the target. 
Shoot Modiﬁers for Ranged Targets 
• Weapon’s Range Modiﬁer. . . . . . . . . . . . . . +/‐ variable 
• Target is a vehicle or mounted model . . . . . . . . . . . +1 
• Target has a Moving Fast marker. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . ‐1 
• Attacker is within close combat range (1") of target .  ‐1 
If any [HIT] results are scored on the dice, the 
target has been hit. 
For example, Zat Smith, a Punk gang member with 
a Shoot stat of 2, ﬁres at a target 7" away using a 
pistol. The pistol is +1 at 8" range or less so Zat rolls 
a total of three Combat dice to determine if he 
scores a hit. 
Zeroes are not heroes 
If modiﬁers reduce a model’s Stat to zero they have 
literally no chance of hitting the chosen target and 
must either select a diﬀerent target or give up on the 
idea of shooting at things for now. 
RANGED ATtACKS 
RESTRICTIONS  
In order to make a ranged attack, a model must have 
the target: 
• within range of the weapon it is using; 
• within its line of sight; 
• within the ﬁre arc of the weapon it is using. 
PICKING A TARGET 
When you make a ranged attack with a model 
nominate a target model on the tabletop and state 
what weapon is being used by the ﬁrer.

## PDF Page 23

21
TAKING HITS – PINnED 
If the total number of [HIT] results rolled by a 
ranged attack is equal to or higher than the 
target’s Cool stat, the target is ‘pinned’ in 
addition to any other results of the hit (i.e. even if the 
hit is subsequently dodged).  
For example, Zat’s target – Bugsy, a lowlife from 
the ‘Stormy Daniels’ block – has Cool 2, so if Zat 
rolls two or more [HIT] results, Bugsy will be pinned 
in addition to any other results. 
When a model is pinned, place a Pinned marker beside 
it as a reminder. 
Eﬀects of being pinned 
When a model with a pinned marker activates roll a 
number of Combat dice equal to the model’s 
Cool stat. Any [SPECIAL] result rolled removes 
the Pinned marker with no further eﬀect. If the 
roll is unsuccessful, the activating model must 
use the single action Shake it Oﬀ! to remove the Pinned 
marker before doing anything else. 
Multiple Pinned markers 
A model can only be pinned once. If a model is already 
pinned and would receive another Pinned marker it 
doesn’t get another one. 
DODGING HITS 
The target model has a chance to dodge the 
incoming hit by rolling a number of Combat 
dice equal to its Evade stat. If any [SPECIAL] 
results are rolled, the hit is dodged and 
entirely negated. 
Evade Modiﬁers for Dodging Ranged Attacks 
• Target attacked by a Incendiary type weapon . . . . . ‐1 
• Target attacked by a Blast type weapon* . . . . . . . . +1 
* Note that this means that even models with no Evade 
get a (small) chance to dodge blasts. 
If a model successfully dodges an incoming hit its owning 
player can move it up to 3" in any direction and change 
its facing as desired. Note that for some Blast weapons 
this means that the model will not be able to fully escape 
the area of eﬀect, even if they dodge successfully. 
RESISTING HITS 
If the hit isn’t dodged it may harm the target. The 
attacker rolls a number of Combat dice equal to the 
weapon’s Power stat to determine the power of the hit.  
= equals one Power. 
= no eﬀect unless the attacker has 
special rules that apply.  
Total up the modiﬁed Power of the attack. 
The target model rolls a number of Combat dice equal 
to its Resist stat. Modify the target’s Resist stat as 
shown below.  
Resist Modiﬁers Against Ranged Attacks 
• Attacker is using two (or more) weapons . . . . . . . . . +1 
• Target model is claiming light cover . . . . . . . . . . . . . +1 
• Target model is claiming heavy cover. . . . . . . . . . . . +2 
• Target model is being attacked in its Back ﬁre arc. . ‐1 
Each [ARMOUR] result rolled reduces the Power 
of the attack by one. 
Shot in the Back or ‘He Never Saw it Coming’ 
In the underworld of the Mega‐City One there is little 
honour among perps and such ‘drokkers’ have no 
hesitation in shooting people in the back. If an incoming 
attack originates in the target’s back ﬁre arc, the 
target’s Resist stat for that hit is reduced by one.

## PDF Page 24

COVER 
Many weapons used in Mega‐City One are powerful 
enough to punch through barriers on a direct hit, this is 
why there are no penalties applied to hit somebody 
lurking behind a crate or wall. It’s not like you only have 
to aim at the exposed parts in order to hit them. 
However, when a model is trying to resist an incoming 
hit, it’s important to know whether or not it can gain 
protection from any cover. Even if the weapon blows 
holes through the cover, there’s less chance of the 
target being hurt so badly if it is hit. The general rules 
for this are simple: 
• If the attacker’s line of sight crosses any kind of cover, 
the defender can claim the beneﬁt of light cover. 
• If the defender is touching a piece of cover that 
the attacker’s line of sight crosses, the defender 
can claim heavy cover instead of light cover. 
Light cover increases the defender’s Resist stat against 
the incoming hit by +1. 
Heavy cover increases the defender’s Resist stat against 
the incoming hit by +2. 
Note: For something to be counted as heavy cover it 
needs to be a reasonably tough material – e.g. metal, 
concrete or plasteel. Lightweight materials, such as 
plastic, synth‐board and plexiglass are limited to 
providing light cover at best. Some powerful and exotic 
weapons like Stub Guns or Laser Riﬂes will simply 
ignore cover altogether. 
 
INJURY CHART 
The result is determined by calculating the attack’s 
Power after it has been modiﬁed by the target’s Resist 
roll. To work out the attack’s eﬀect, refer to the Injury 
Chart below.  
INJURY CHART 
No eﬀect 
The hit deﬂects harmlessly from a fashionable kneepad, 
piece of armour, personal electronic device or other 
harmless trinket. 
22

‐1 or less
No eﬀect

The target is stunned

The target is injured
2
The target is seriously injured
3
The target is grievously injured
THE GUNFIGHTER SKILl 
Some individuals are either naturally gifted 
gunﬁghters or highly trained in gunplay through 
years of practice in the Justice Department, Citi‐
Def or the Block Gangs.  
When a model with the Gunﬁghter skill 
successfully dodges a ranged attack or rolls 
a [SPECIAL] result on its Resist roll against a 
ranged combat attack, it can get a return Snapshot 
oﬀ at its attacker.  
In order to shoot back, the model must: 
• not be pinned (either already or by the 
incoming attack); 
• have its attacker in its Front ﬁre arc. (FF if not 
using a pistol); 
• have a ranged weapon that can be used for  
a Snapshot. 
If all these criteria are met, resolve a Snapshot from 
the defending model against its attacker before 
resolving the damage from the hit against the 
defender. Note that rolling a [SPECIAL] result does 
nothing to resist the damage (unless the model has 
a special rule that says it does), it just grants an 
opportunity for ﬁring back. 
The results of both attacks are worked  
out simultaneously. 
BULlET TIME 
If both models have the Gunﬁghter skill, the counter‐
shot from the defender can potentially trigger 
another attack from the original attacker if 
the attacker successfully dodges or rolls a 
[SPECIAL] result on its Resist roll and fulﬁls all 
of the three criteria given above. 
As long as models keep rolling  
[SPECIAL] for the Resist roll, they keep exchanging 
shots until one of them (potentially both) is pinned 
or becomes incapacitated by a hit.

## PDF Page 25

For example, the aforementioned lowlife Bugsy  
has a Cool of 2. If he receives two Injury markers, 
he’s incapacitated and out of the ﬁght. 
SUBDUED 
A model that is reduced to zero Cool by Stun markers 
only is said to been subdued instead of incapacitated. 
This is important to note for when you need to 
sentence perps – the subdued ones have a better 
chance of ending up in iso‐cubes rather than in Resyk. 
If playing in a campaign, for the purpose of checking 
their survival later, keep note of whether a model was 
incapacitated or subdued at the end of the game .  
Bugsy receives two Stun markers so he is subdued 
and out of the ﬁght. If he received one Stun marker 
and one Injury marker, he’d be incapacitated instead. 
Stunned 
Place a Stunned marker on the target model. All of the 
stunned model’s stats, including Move, are reduced by 
one for each Stunned marker it has sustained, until the 
model uses a Shake it Oﬀ! action to remove the 
Stunned marker (see page 17). 
Injured 
Place an Injury marker on the target model. For the rest 
of the game, all of the model’s stats, including Move, 
are reduced by one for each Injury marker it has. Injury 
markers can be removed by taking a Hunker Down 
double‐action. 
Seriously injured 
Place two Injury markers on the target model. All of the 
injured model’s stats, including Move, are reduced by 
one for each Injury marker it has sustained. Injury 
markers can be removed by taking a Hunker Down 
double‐action. 
Grievously injured 
Place three Injury markers on the target model. All of 
the Injured model’s stats are reduced by one for each 
Injury marker it has sustained. Injury levels can be 
reduced by taking a Hunker Down double‐action. 
INCAPACITATED - A CANDIDATE FOR RESYK 
Injuries will accumulate on a model and beyond a 
certain point a model will be classed as incapacitated 
and out of the game.  
If a model accumulates so many Stunned and/or Injury 
markers that its Cool stat is reduced to zero, it counts as 
incapacitated, meaning it’s been rendered unconscious 
(or dead) and is at least temporarily out of the game. 
Models that are incapacitated don’t put Action or Star 
chips in the bag and can take no actions. Some Armoury 
and Big Meg cards, plus certain skills can be used to 
revive models that are incapacitated. 
23
RESYK  
Here the eleven million citizens who die 
each year find a last resting place, sort of. 
Once through the plush synthi-velv curtains, 
mechanical arms remove the corpses and 
place them on the conveyor belt. They are 
then joined by others from smaller Resyk 
terminals dotted all over the city, as they 
flow smoothly into the recycling plant. Here, 
over a thousand corpses per hour are 
dissected and broken down into 107 useful 
constituents. In the post-nuclear world, 
nothing can be wasted – and the valuable 
chemicals contained in the human body are 
vital to the city's hungry industries. The 
Resyk workers have a proud boast: “We use 
everything but the soul!"
Core Rules

## PDF Page 26

CLOSE COMBAT 
Models within 1" of each other are within close  
combat range. Blades, blunt objects, ﬁsts or even  
teeth become the weapon of convenience.  
However, a model can still choose to shoot at  
enemies in close combat range, although several 
restrictions apply:  
• There is a penalty of ‐1 to Shoot at close  
combat range.  
• A model may not choose to use Aimed Fire when 
an enemy is within close combat range. 
Overall, most of the time the better option is to take a 
Fight action and grapple or batter the oﬀending party 
(however, be aware results may vary depending on how 
good your model is at ﬁghting). See page 26 for the 
option of just running away. 
FIGHT! 
A Fight action or a Charge action which brings the 
active model within close combat range (1") of an 
enemy model is resolved as follows. 
HITtING THE ENEMY 
Roll a number of Combat dice equal to the 
attacker’s Fight stat to see if a hit is scored.  
If there are any [HIT] results, it means the 
attacker has landed a blow of some form. 
Modiﬁers to Fight for Close Combat Attacks  
• The target is pinned . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . +1  
• Attacking model was in the  
target’s Back ﬁre arc (B) when it activated* . . . . . . +1 
* You can’t start in front of an enemy and then run behind 
it to make the attack to gain this bonus. 
24
PINnING  
Successive rains of kicks or blows can suppress 
an enemy just as much as a hail of shots. If the 
close combat attack rolls a number of [HIT] 
results equal to or greater than the defender’s Cool, 
place a Pinned marker on the defender.  
DODGING  
The defender can attempt to dodge a close 
combat attack by rolling a number of Combat 
dice equal to its Evade stat. If any [SPECIAL] 
result is rolled, the defender has dodged the hit and 
takes no damage.  
Note that a model that successfully dodges an 
incoming hit can be moved up to 3" by its owning 
player, so it has an opportunity to move out of close 
combat range if desired.

## PDF Page 28

ESCAPING CLOSE COMBAT  
A model within close combat range of an enemy is able 
to move away using a Move, Sprint or Charge action 
when it activates. Compare the active model’s Move to 
the models that are in close combat range of it. Any 
enemies with a higher Move stat than the active 
model get to make an immediate close 
combat attack against the ﬂeeing model 
with no chance of a counter‐attack. 
THE BRAWLER SKILl 
The streets of Mega‐City One can be an 
extremely dangerous place and hand‐to‐hand 
combat is commonplace, whether it is a simple 
mugging or a full‐on rumble between Juve 
gangs. Those individuals particularly competent 
in hand‐to‐hand are generically called Brawlers.  
If a defending model with the Brawler 
skill successfully dodges or rolls a 
[SPECIAL] result on its Combat dice to 
resist a close combat hit, it can make a 
counter‐attack by striking back. Resolve this 
simultaneously as another Fight action 
initiated by the defender (i.e. before applying 
any result from the original attack).  
If both models have the Brawler skill, the 
counter‐hit from the defender can potentially 
trigger another attack from the original 
attacker if the attacker successfully dodges or 
rolls a [SPECIAL] result on its Resist roll.  
As long as models keep rolling [SPECIAL] results 
for their Resist roll, they keep exchanging hits 
until one of them (potentially both) is pinned 
or becomes incapacitated or subdued by a hit. 
SUBDUING IN CLOSE COMBAT 
A close combat attack made without using a speciﬁc 
weapon (i.e. using only your ﬁsts, feet or head) is always 
considered to have the Stun Special Rule (e.g. the 
attacker has the option to inﬂict at least one stunned 
result and count all injuries as stuns – see page 49). 
However, close combat weapons without the Stun Special 
Rule may not be used for subduing for obvious reasons. 
PINnED COMBATANTS 
If a close combat attack is made against a pinned model, 
the attacker gains +1 Fight and there is no chance for a 
defender with the Brawler skill to counter‐attack. The 
close combat attack is otherwise unaﬀected. 
MULTIPLE OPpONENTS 
Multiple attackers ganging up onto single opponents 
generally tend to get in each other’s way, so no bonuses 
apply for outnumbering.  
The one exception is models that operate in 
packs. Packs such as groups of Juves score hits 
in close combat on both [HIT] and [SPECIAL] 
results if at least one other member of the pack 
is also within close combat range of the same 
target model. 
A close combat attacker with multiple enemy models in 
range can elect to split its Fight stat in order to attack 
more than one model. At least one point of Fight must be 
assigned to each model being attacked and the allocated 
Fight is used to determine the Power of the attack. 
26


---

## PDF Page 29

TWO-WEAPON FIGHTING  
A model can attack with a close combat weapon in each 
hand (providing that both weapons are one handed of 
course). When a model uses two close combat 
weapons in this way it will score hits on the 
target with both [HIT] and [SPECIAL] results on 
its Combat dice. The ﬂurry of blows struck are 
individually less weighty so the target model 
gains +1 Resist dice if hit. 
Having a pistol in one hand and a close combat weapon 
in another also counts for this rule. Power for close 
combat hits uses either the model’s Fight stat plus 
modiﬁers and Special Rules for one of its close combat 
weapon or the pistol’s Power and Special Rules. If the 
attacker is splitting their Fight in order to attack 
multiple opponents (see Multiple Opponents) only one 
opponent can be attacked using the pistol’s Power.  
Core Rules

## PDF Page 30

MOUNTED MODELS  
Sometimes you need to get places fast. Models can be 
mounted on various wheeled or grav assisted mounts, 
jet packs, or even winged bat suits to get around.  
Typical examples include powerful motorcycles that are 
the mount of choice for the numerous bike gangs that 
plague Mega‐City One. Whilst above the streets daring 
sky boarders, like the famous Chopper, ﬂaunt the law 
on anti‐gravity power boards.  
Whilst a model is mounted it 
gains certain stat 
modiﬁers 
depending on 
the type of 
mount. As we 
can see from the 
examples below, the 
beneﬁts of increased 
mobility are often dampened 
by the impediment of not 
being able to shoot or ﬁght 
quite as well. 
MOUNTING AND DISMOUNTING  
Mounting or dismounting takes a single action by a 
model. A dismounting model can be placed anywhere 
within 1" of its mount. If a dismounting model is placed 
within 1" of an enemy, it can make a close combat 
attack as part of its dismount to represent diving oﬀ 
into combat. 
A model must be within 1" of an unoccupied mount to 
mount it. Once a model has mounted, it gains the stat 
bonuses of its mount. Once a model dismounts for any 
reason, it reverts to its normal stat line. 
ATtACKING MOUNTED MODELS 
Mounted models are larger targets, so ranged attacks 
against them gain +1 to the attacker’s Shoot stat.  
Drive-by close combat attacks while mounted 
When a mounted model uses a Charge double‐action, it 
can make a close combat attack at any point during its 
movement instead of just at the end.  
Forced dismount 
A mounted model that is pinned, stunned or injured by 
an attack (ranged or close combat) must pass a Cool test 
or be forcibly dismounted. Roll a number of Combat dice 
equal to the model’s Cool stat (remember it will 
be modiﬁed by Stun and Injury markers). If any 
[SPECIAL] results are rolled, the model remains 
mounted; otherwise it is dismounted and gains 
an additional Stunned marker. Incapacitated 
and subdued models automatically fail this test.

29
Roll a Combat die for the mount. On a  
[HIT] result, the mount is destroyed. On any 
other result, the mount survives and may 
potentially be remounted. 
The Grav trait and forced dismounts  
A forced dismount from a Grav mount will also result in 
a potentially damaging fall to the ground. Resolve a 
Power 3 hit against the rider to represent this. 
In case you were wondering, Citizen, the remount 
action required for Bat Gliders or Jet Packs which are 
worn rather than ridden represents preparing the gear 
for ﬂight again. 
UNATtENDED MOUNTS 
Use markers to represent unattended mounts if you 
are not blessed with spare models of the right kind. 
An active model that gets within 1" of an unattended 
mount can elect to spend an action to remove it from 
play, representing the mount being sabotaged. 
GRAV  
Anti‐Gravity or ‘Grav’ technology is very common in 
Mega‐City One. Grav mounts are capable of floating 
anywhere from a few metres to several hundred 
metres above the ground.  
In the context of this skirmish game, a model on a 
Grav mount when moving can ignore any models less 
than 6" in height or ignore Blocking terrain that has a 
height less than that of its Move stat.  
The mounted model must still end its movement in 
terrain passable to it.    
ONE-HANDED WEAPON ONLY 
Riders of mounts with this trait may only use one‐handed 
weapons or weapons with the Throw Special Rule.  
Due to bracing issues, riders of mounts may not use 
weapons classed as Heavy Weapons

## PDF Page 32

VEHICLES  
Whilst large areas of Mega‐City One are pedestrianised, 
numerous roads and streets ranging from small 
pipeways and zipstrips to massive Skedways and 
Megways also criss‐cross the city.  
Hence it is not uncommon for vehicles to appear in 
Judge Dredd games, although they are often subject to 
dramatic, ﬁery destruction if they do. In practice, 
vehicles generally make excellent terrain for breaking 
up a gunﬁght. However, they can also add interesting 
variety to a game and one scenario in this book, Mo‐
pad Madness, is written with vehicles in mind. 
A vehicle is activated like a model and shares many 
stats with models on foot. However, several restrictions 
apply to vehicle activation and movement. 
ACTIVATION 
Vehicles activate and take actions just like other 
models. However, vehicles without an A.I. do not 
create Action chips of their own so they have to be 
activated by someone else’s Action chip. Typically, this 
is a passenger acting as the driver, although not 
necessarily thanks to remote driving technologies. 
A vehicle capable of full A.I. (Artiﬁcial Intelligence) 
control (i.e. it generates its own Action chip) will be 
noted in its statistics. 
Regardless of whether being driven by a passenger or 
not, a vehicle uses its own stats, not the theoretical 
driver’s, when taking actions or being subject to attack. 
Vehicles may only choose the following actions:  
• Move. 
• Accelerate. 
• Decelerate. 
• Snapshot (if armed). 
MOVEMENT  
A stationary vehicle must use a unique single action –
Accelerate – before it can declare a Move action. Place 
a Moving Fast marker on the vehicle and then perform 
the Move as a separate action. 
When a vehicle activates, its ﬁrst action must be Move 
if it has a Moving Fast marker on it. A second Move 
action may then be taken if desired. 
A moving vehicle can use a unique action – 
Decelerate –after its ﬁrst Move action to remove the 
Moving Fast marker from the vehicle and make it 
stationary again. 
Turning 
Vehicles must use a turn radius if they don’t want to just 
travel forward in a straight line, and the bigger the 
vehicle, the bigger the turn radius. 
A vehicle’s turn radius is equal to the vehicle model’s 
length plus its width (measure the widest points on the 
vehicle, round up any fractions). A vehicle can turn for 
as much of its Move as desired but is always subject to 
the turn radius. 
Measure the turn radius from 
the vehicle’s front corner on 
the side it is turning towards. 
Turn Radius 
A vehicle that is 3” 
long (A) and 2.5” wide 
(B) has a turning 
radius of 6” (A+B 
rounded up).
A
Turn in place/reverse 
A vehicle that doesn’t have a Moving Fast marker on it 
can expend a single action to turn in place up to 2" or 
move in reverse up to 25% of its Move stat. 
B

## PDF Page 33

GRAV VEHICLES 
Anti‐Gravity technology is common in the 22nd century 
and has revolutionised transportation allowing vehicles 
to ‘skim’ or ﬂy for short distances. Vehicles with the 
Grav special rule are capable of ﬂying above obstacles if 
desired. When a grav vehicle moves, it can ignore any 
models or terrain as it does so up to a height equal to 
the vehicle’s Move stat. 
For example, a Grav Pod (Move 12") can reach up to 
12" in height as it moves. 
We know that some grav vehicles in Mega‐City One can 
ﬂy very high, H‐Wagons for example, but in the interests 
of simplicity, we will restrict this for the game.  
PASsENGERS 
All vehicles can carry at least two passengers in a 
modicum of comfort (if they carry only one person, they 
are classiﬁed as a mount rather than a vehicle) but 
some can carry many more.  
An activated model within 1" of a vehicle with empty 
passenger seats can expend a single action to get 
aboard it and occupy an empty passenger seat.  
A passenger may disembark from a vehicle by 
expending an single action when activated. The model 
is then placed within 1" of the vehicle.  
Road rash 
If a vehicle has a Moving Fast marker when a model 
embarks or disembarks from it, the model must pass a 
Cool test to do so safely. If the model fails the test it, 
suﬀers a ram attack from the vehicle and is then placed 
within 1" of the vehicle.  
Knock knock 
Note that a model doesn’t have to be friendly to a 
vehicle to get aboard. A model that embarks onto a 
hostile vehicle gets to make a free close combat attack 
against another passenger aboard the vehicle if desired. 
If a model disembarks from a vehicle and is placed 
within 1" of an enemy model, the disembarking model 
can make a close combat attack if desired. 
VEHICLES ATtACKING 
Justice Department and other militarised vehicles may 
have weapons mounted on them. In these cases, the 
vehicle’s proﬁle will have its own Shoot stat. More 
often, passengers will use Snapshot or Aimed Fire 
actions from a vehicle when they are activated. Ranged 
attacks from vehicles and passengers suﬀer the 
following additional Shoot modiﬁer: 
Shoot modiﬁer for ranged attacks 
• Attacker is a vehicle or a passenger  
aboard a vehicle with a Moving Fast marker . . . . . . ‐1 
RAM ATtACK 
When a vehicle model is moved, it executes a ram 
attack against any obstacles (terrain) or models 
(victims) in its path. 
Note that a Grav vehicles may choose to avoid ramming 
any object or model that has a height less than that of 
its Move statistic.  
Use the vehicle’s Fight stat to resolve a close combat 
attack against anything it rams. 
Models gain the following modiﬁers to Evade against 
ram attacks:  
Evade modiﬁers for dodging attacks 
• Target attacked by a vehicle ram* . . . . . . . . . . . . . . +1 
* Note that this means that even models with no Evade 
stat get a chance to dodge vehicles. 
If a vehicle rams an obstacle and fails to remove it, the 
vehicle’s move is stopped and it potentially suﬀers 
damage (see Crunch! below).

## PDF Page 34

ATtACKING VEHICLES 
Shooting at a vehicle always gains the +1 Shoot 
modiﬁer for a large target. 
Shoot modiﬁers for ranged attacks 
• Target is a vehicle. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . +1 
Close Combat 
Close combat attacks can be attempted against a 
vehicle but are risky if the vehicle is Moving Fast. Take a 
Cool test before making the attack if a vehicle is Moving 
Fast. The close combat attack is resolved whether the 
test is passed or failed, but if it is failed the vehicle also 
makes a ram attack against the attacking model. 
Pinned vehicles 
If a vehicle is pinned by an attack (i.e. number  
of [HITS] rolled to hit equals or beats the vehicle’s 
Cool) place a Pinned marker on the vehicle. 
When a vehicle with Pinned marker activates, make a 
Cool test to see if it can remove the Pinned marker 
before taking an action as normal. 
If the test is failed and the vehicle is stationary, the 
vehicle must simply expend an action to remove the 
Pinned marker before taking any other actions. 
If the test is failed and the vehicle is marked with a 
Moving Fast marker, life gets more interesting: The 
vehicle is out of control! 
Out of control! 
A vehicle that is pinned while it has a Moving Fast 
marker is no longer fully in control. Roll a D6 to 
determine each of its actions for this activation. 
Note that grav vehicles lose their ability to ignore 
obstacles they move over while they are out of control. 
Crunch! 
If an out of control vehicle encounters an obstacle it 
cannot move over or ram out of the way, a hit is 
inﬂicted against the vehicle using its own Resist stat as 
the Power. The vehicle’s move is halted, and its Moving 
Fast marker is removed. 
DAMAGE TO VEHICLES 
Vehicles have special damage tables for being hit: 
No eﬀect 
The hit deﬂects harmlessly from a non‐essential and 
entirely cosmetic part of the vehicle such as a spoiler or 
hood ornament. 
Passengers Stunned 
The hit throws the occupants around. Place one 
Stunned marker on every passenger aboard the vehicle. 
The vehicle itself is unaﬀected. 
Damaged 
Place an Injury marker on the vehicle. For the rest of 
the game, all of the vehicle’s stats are reduced by one 
for each Injury marker it has sustained. The 
vehicle may only make Move or Accelerate 
actions when it activates if a [HIT] is rolled on 
one Combat dice before each action. 
Passengers are unharmed. 
Immobilised 
Place two Injury markers on the vehicle. For the rest of 
the game, all of the vehicle’s stats are reduced by one 
for each Injury marker it has sustained. The vehicle may 
no longer take Move or Accelerate actions. If the vehicle 
has a Moving Fast marker on it at the point when it was 
immobilised, all passengers suﬀer a hit with Power 
equal to the vehicle’s Fight stat. 
Destroyed 
The vehicle is wrecked and no longer functions.  
All passengers suﬀer a hit with Power equal to the 
vehicle’s Resist stat. If the vehicle has a Moving Fast 
marker on it at the point when it was destroyed, 
passengers suﬀer two hits instead of one. Resolve 
each hit separately.

## PDF Page 36

TERrAIN 
In Judge Dredd terrain refers to any features on the 
tabletop like buildings, walls, barriers, wrecks and 
immobile vehicles that our protagonists must ﬁght in, 
around or over. Terrain helps to deﬁne the tabletop 
arena by presenting challenges and opportunities for 
both sides, so it’s vital for a good game. Terrain might 
be represented simply by using card markers to 
indicate its type and location or with sumptuous three‐
dimensional dioramas of streets, blocks, malls, burnt 
out ruins, scrapyards, rad pits and other Mega‐City 
One urban features.  
Terrain aﬀects games of Judge Dredd in two important 
ways. Firstly, how it aﬀects movement and secondly 
what Resist beneﬁts it gives to a model using it for 
cover. Its Resist stat also determines how diﬃcult it is to 
blow holes through or even destroy it.  
DEFINING TERrAIN AREAS 
A tricky aspect of ruling on terrain can be deciding 
where it begins and ends. Sometimes it can be helpful 
to think of terrain in terms of whole areas rather than 
individual points. For example, you might deﬁne the 
outer boundary of an area of wrecked buildings and say 
that any model within that boundary is aﬀected by the 
terrain rules for those ruins. Alternately, you could treat 
each wall or pile of rubble as a separate 
piece of terrain and 
allow models to navigate between them without being 
aﬀected by the terrain.  
Either method is valid, providing it is consistent and 
both players agree and understand it. The best 
approach depends on the size of the game and the 
kinds of terrain you have access to. As a rule, the larger 
the game the more you should lean towards 
designating larger areas of terrain for ease of play. It’s a 
good idea to prevent any in‐game confusion by 
discussing the battleﬁeld terrain with your opponent 
before starting the game. 
For the purposes of movement, we classify terrain 
into three types: Open terrain, Diﬃcult terrain, and 
Blocking terrain. 
34
TERrAIN MOVEMENT SUMmARY 
•
Open terrain can be ignored  
while moving. 
•
Diﬃcult terrain reduces model moves 
by 50%. 
•
Blocking terrain blocks all movement 


---

## PDF Page 37

OPEN TERrAIN 
Open terrain is classiﬁed as any with features that are 
less than 1" tall and up to 1" across. These can be 
ignored by models while moving on the assumption 
that they can easily duck around, sidestep, or vault over 
them without slowing down.  
Examples of Open terrain areas might include:  
• City streets or roadways; 
• Pedestrianised areas; 
• Roof tops; 
• Sports ﬁelds; 
• The interiors of large buildings or city blocks, such 
as malls, food courts or open recreation areas. 
OPEN TERrAIN OBSTACLES 
Most open areas can be scattered with various 
obstacles such as crates, oil drums, stalls, vending 
machines, videophone booths, furniture and low walls 
or similar barriers 1" or less in height. 
Vehicles and mounted models in Open terrain 
Vehicles and mounted models may not completely 
ignore Open terrain and must move around any 
obstacles instead. In eﬀect, these obstacles are 
Blocking terrain for vehicles and mounted models. 
Vehicles or mounts with the Grav trait may ignore Open 
terrain obstacles simply ﬂying over them.  
DIFfICULT TERrAIN 
Diﬃcult terrain is anything that impedes, but does not 
completely block, movement, meaning it can be 
traversed with some eﬀort.  
Examples of Diﬃcult terrain areas include:  
• Dense rubble, broken ground, or craters. 
• Obstacles like walls, fences or other barriers 
between 1" and 2" tall. 
• Shallow water courses, such as can be found in 
the Undercity and sewer levels. 
• A street crowded with citizens and other  
light traﬃc. 
A model’s current Move stat is reduced by 50% 
(rounding up) if it moves across or through any terrain 
that is rated Diﬃcult during a Move action. A model 
cannot use Sprint or Charge double‐actions if it intends 
to cross any Diﬃcult terrain. 
For example, a model with Move stat of 6" crosses a 
2" high partition during its Move action. The diﬃcult 
terrain reduces the model’s Move stat to 3" for this 
Move action. 
Note that a Move action is reduced if any part of it takes 
place in or crosses Diﬃcult terrain, whether at the 
beginning, middle, or end. However, each action is 
assessed separately so, for example, it is possible to use 
a Move action to get out of Diﬃcult terrain and then 
use another Move action in the Open terrain beyond 
immediately afterwards. 
Vehicles & mounted models in diﬃcult terrain  
Vehicles and mounted models suﬀer the same 50% 
Move penalty as models on foot when crossing Diﬃcult 
terrain like shallow water or rubble but they treat all 
obstacles as Blocking terrain. Vehicles or mounts with 
the Grav trait may ignore Diﬃcult terrain obstacles.  
Fancy footwork 
A model with an Evade stat can use some fancy 
footwork to try and ignore the eﬀects of Diﬃcult terrain 
when it moves. Roll an Evade test at the start 
of the model’s Move action. If the model scores 
any [HIT] results it ignores the Diﬃcult terrain 
for its action. If the test is failed the Diﬃcult 
terrain aﬀects the model’s Move as normal, reducing 
the model’s Move to 50% of its stat. 
BLOCKING TERrAIN 
Blocking terrain cannot be moved through at all – 
though it may possibly be moved over or around it by 
using the Leap/Climb double‐action (see page 36). 
Examples of Blocking terrain include:  
• Buildings. 
• Shipping containers. 
• Large vehicles or vehicle wrecks. 
• Obstacles including walls or barriers that are more 
than 2" tall. 
• Deep water. 
• Gaps between buildings if the models are  
moving across the roofs. 
For example, a model is confronted 
by a 3" high perimeter wall, it may 
not move through the wall and so 
must go around it instead. 
Grav vehicles or mounts may 
choose to ignore blocking terrain 
that has a height less than that of 
their Move stat.  
Core Rules
35


---

## PDF Page 38

LEAPING/CLIMBING  
A model using the Leap/Climb double‐action eﬀectively 
circumvents movement penalties from terrain. The 
model can move up to its Move distance in any 
direction, including upwards, to get over or on top of 
obstacles that they otherwise can’t pass through. 
LADdERS AND STAIRS 
If an obstacle features ladders or stairs, a model may 
use a standard Move or Sprint double‐action to move 
upwards or downwards to get over or on top of 
obstacles they normally cannot pass through. Each 1" of 
vertical distance costs 1" of movement for conventional 
ladders and stairs, if using escalators, elevators or grav‐
tubes vertical distance changes are free.  
FALlING  
If there’s elevation of any kind you can almost 
guarantee someone’s going to try and take 
the high ground… and then fall off it. Models 
that are pinned, injured or stunned when 
within 1" of a ledge or drop have a chance of 
falling. Take a Cool test for the model, if it fails 
to roll any [SPECIAL] or [ARMOUR] results it falls. 
A model that falls is moved to the bottom of wherever 
it fell, is pinned and suﬀers a hit with a cumulative 
Power of 2 for each 2" fallen (rounding up). 
For example, the unlucky Zat Smith suﬀers an Injury 
whilst ﬁring from the roof of a 6" high building. Zat 
tests against his Cool. He fails to roll any [SPECIAL] 
or [ARMOUR] and plummets towards the hard 
ferrocrete below! He is immediately pinned and 
suﬀers a Power 6 hit. Thud! 
JUMPING BETWEeN BUILDINGS 
If a model uses the Leaping/Climbing double‐action to 
circumvent the Blocking terrain eﬀect of gaps between 
rooftops, roll an Evade test at the start of the model’s 
Move action. Add +1 Combat die to the test ,so even a 
model with no evade stat may roll one Combat die for 
this test.  
Note that a model using the Leaping/Climbing  
double‐action may not attempt to cross a gap wider 
than its Move statistic.  
If the model scores any [HIT] results it 
successfully crosses the gap. If the test is failed 
the model stops moving at the gap and is 
subject to the falling rules above.  
TERrAIN RESIST STATISTICS 
There are going to be times when terrain is going to 
get blasted by weapons or rammed by vehicles.  
For these purposes, terrain is given a Resist stat as 
noted below.  
Note that creative players will no doubt populate  
their tabletop with a fine range of varied terrain 
features. Use the below suggestions of terrain Resist 
stats as a guide.  
• Flimsy: Oﬃce partitions, shelves, wooden or 
plastic fencing, City Block interior doors, windows, 
crates, etc. Resist 1. 
• Solid: Hastily built barricades built from street 
debris, reinforced City Block apartment entrance 
doors, City Block internal walls, vending machines. 
Resist 4. 
• Sturdy: External City Block walls, purpose built 
defensive barriers, wrecked vehicles. Resist 8. 
Resolve hits against terrain using the attack’s 
Power versus the terrain’s Resist. For each [HIT] 
of Power rolled by the attack that the terrain 
fails to Resist by rolling [ARMOUR] results, a 
roughly 1" block of terrain can be removed or 
pushed out of the way. 
For example, a Ground Car (Fight 3) rams a ﬂimsy 
fence (Resist 1) and rolls three Combat die scoring 
two [HITS], the fence rolls 1 die but fails to roll an 
[ARMOUR] result so the fence is crushed or knocked 
aside. The fence now has a 2" gap in it.  
36


---

## PDF Page 39

VEHICLES RAMmING OBSTACLES  
If a vehicle either fails to completely remove the 
obstacle, or the hole created is not large enough for the 
vehicle to pass through, then it is subject to Crunch! as 
detailed in the vehicle rules on page 32. 
FIGHTING INSIDE BUILDINGS 
Whilst some players may choose to just use buildings as 
solid blocking terrain and assume models do not enter 
them, others may possess buildings with removable 
roofs or storeys and want to place models inside them 
and move them around.  
If you don’t have buildings with detailed interiors, it’s also 
possible to have a separate ﬂoor plan preferably made 
with a 1" grid and move the action to the plan when 
models enter the buildings or when you want to indicate 
which windows or doors occupiers are ﬁring from.  
DOoRS AND WINDOWS 
Unless the scenario speciﬁes otherwise, all doors and 
windows are assumed to be secured. Whilst they can of 
course be blasted open by gunﬁre, they can also be 
broken open by a model that declares a Charge action 
that contacts the door or window.  
Resolve the Charge by rolling the model’s Fight 
stat (+1 for charging) and rolling the door or 
window’s Resist stat. If the number of [HIT] 
results of the Fight roll exceeds the number of 
[ARMOUR] results of the Resist roll the door or 
window is assumed to be smashed open.  
The charging model may then complete its move but 
it must pass through the opening. It may enter close 
combat with a model on the other side of the door or 
window if it can move within 1" of it. 
If the Resist roll scores a number of [Armour] 
results equal to or in excess of the charger’s 
[Hit] results, from their Fight roll, the door 
holds ﬁrm and the charging model immediately 
stops moving. In addition to being the centre of 
much hilarity, the model is pinned and receives 
one Stun marker.  
Example: Mean Machine Angel charges a block's 
apartment door, behind which an unfortunate 
Citi‐Def trooper lies in wait. Mean charges into 
contact with the door and rolls 5 dice for his Fight 
stat. He rolls 3 x [HIT] results. The Door has a 
Resist of 4 so that many Combat dice are rolled. 
Only 2 [ARMOUR] results are rolled so the door 
succumbs and Mean crashes through. After 
completing his charge move, Mean is within 1" of 
the Citi‐Def trooper and engages him in close 
combat. BOK!!!!!!!! 
Picking the lock 
Alternatively, a locked door or window can be opened 
with a bit of skill and luck. A character using the Hunker 
Down action can attempt to open any single 
locked door or window within 1" by rolling a 
Cool test. If a [SPECIAL] result is rolled, the 
window or door is opened without triggering 
any alarms. When designing scenarios, you can of 
course deliberately reduce the number of Cool dice 
rolled by the character. For example, to simulate a 
triple‐locked mechanism you might reduce the number 
of dice rolled in the test by ‐1. 
37
SLEeP MACHINES 
Most Judges find it extremely 
hard to relax and grab some 
sleep after an adrenalin-
soaked patrol on the street.  
The Sleep machines found in 
every sector house allow a 
Judge rest, whatever the 
situation. Electromagnetic 
pulses simulate the brains 
natural sleep patterns putting 
the Judge into a deep and 
natural sleep. So effective is 
this process that ten minutes 
in the Sleep Machine gives the same recuperative rest as eight hours of natural sleep. Of 
course, the accounts division has also seen the benefits as Judges can now spend twenty-
three hours on duty solid. Truly cost effective law enforcement.

## PDF Page 41

Unlawful possession of weapons within the conﬁnes of 
Mega‐City One is taken very seriously by the Justice 
Department. According to the Chief Judge and the 
Council of Five, the old United States’ Constitutional 
promise of the ‘right to bear arms’ is fulﬁlled by the Citi‐
Def voluntary militia program and thus citizens have no 
need for personal weapons. Those individuals that claim 
they do need weapons for protection, like corporate 
security or private investigators, are required to secure 
expensive, hard‐to‐obtain permits.  
Still, there are estimated to be tens of millions of 
unregistered weapons in circulation in the city and 
there is a constant demand for more. Armed crime is 
prevalent in Mega‐City One, and many gang disputes 
soon escalate to the stage where bullets begin to ﬂy. 
Both those who wish to commit crimes and those who 
do not wish to commit their personal protection to the 
tender mercies of the Judges exist in a shadowy, self‐
sustaining arms race.  
Some of these weapons are surplus leftovers from the 
wars of the 21st century, others are ‘mislaid’ Citi‐Def 
weapons, family heirlooms or genuine 20th century 
antiques. There is also an extensive black market  
for weapons illegally imported from elsewhere on  
Earth and even from oﬀ‐world. A signiﬁcant number  
of the weapons seized by Judges come from 
underground workshops within the city itself producing 
low quality, yet deadly, weapons. Yet more are 
clandestinely shipped in from mutie settlements out in 
the Cursed Earth. 
Whilst advanced technological ﬁrearms such as Lasers 
and Disintegrators do exist, the most commonly used 
weapons are slug throwers using chemical propellants 
that would be familiar to any student of pre‐Atomic 
history. Many of the weapons used in the Mega‐City are 
almost indistinguishable from their late 20th and early 
21st century predecessors. Indeed, certain gangs make 
it a part of their style to use replicas of earlier weapons 
like ‘six‐shooters’ or ‘Tommy guns’.  
The Armoury pages that follow cover rules for a wide 
variety of weaponry for Judge Dredd including heavy 
weapons and bombs. 
ARMOURY
THE LONG WALK 
When a Judge has exhibited an error of judgement that the 
SJS considers not warranting a criminal penalty, rather 
than being forced out of the department they can opt to 
take the Long Walk.  
Equipped with a Lawgiver and a rifle of their own choice, 
the Judge leaves Mega-City One and walks alone into the 
Cursed Earth, to bring justice to that lawless realm. If the 
retirement is honourable the Judge’s walk out of the city 
is marked by an Honour Guard firing a Lawgiver volley in 
salute. The survival rate for most Judges is mere weeks or 
months. Whilst a few like Dredd and McGruder have 
returned, they are exceptional cases.

MAXIMUM WEAPONS CARrIED 
A model can normally carry up to three ‘hands’ 
of weapons. For example:  
• Three pistols or close combat weapons, 
or a mix thereof (each one‐handed); 
or… 
• One heavy weapon or close combat 
weapon (two‐handed) or ranged weapon 
(two‐handed), plus one pistol or close 
combat weapon (one‐handed). 
Judges or aliens, robots and mutants with 
multiple appendages can carry more weapons 
than this – this will be stipulated in their 
individual rules.
USING MULTIPLE GUNS 
Certain skilled individuals, or spuggers just  
eager to dish out more ﬁrepower, may enter 
combat with a gun in each hand. When a 
Snapshot is taken, a model may ﬁre several guns 
together if it has the limbs and weapons 
available. The following rules apply: 
• Only one‐handed ranged weapons or 
Sawn‐oﬀ Stump guns may be used. 
• The model’s ﬁre arc becomes Focused 
Front (FF) for the Snapshot. 
The attacking model adds +1 Shoot per 
additional weapon ﬁred that is within range and 
picks one weapon used to supply the Range 
Modiﬁer. If a hit is scored, use the lowest Power 
of the weapons being used if they are diﬀerent.  
The target gains a single +1 Resist against the 
multi‐gun Snapshot due to the ‘quantity over 
quality’ of the shooting.

PISTOLS 
Pistols are compact weapons that can be carried and 
ﬁred with one hand. The majority of pistols in the Big 
Meg are slug throwers, shooting conventional bullets 
propelled by chemical cartridges. Directed energy laser 
pistols are becoming more popular but are expensive 
and somewhat fragile.
