## PDF Page 135

133
THE SET UP 
Among the densely packed, mostly angry citizens of the 
Big Meg, a tense situation can escalate into a full‐blown 
battle royale at the drop of a freezy‐whip. Rumbles are 
often the result of a high‐tension face‐oﬀ between rival 
factions, criminals, Judges or even entire City Blocks that 
explodes into violence. Rumble is suitable for any size of 
games. Vehicles are permitted in a Rumble scenario.  
THE ENCOUNTER 
Terrain Placement  
The table must include a cross roads with streets 12"‐18" 
wide running across the table from each side to the other. 
The street should be open and clear of any buildings but 
could still include barriers, fast‐food stands, seating areas, 
trash compactors, parked vehicles, etc. The areas in the 
four corner sections can have buildings but may include 
parks, parking lots, waste ground, rad pits, etc. 
Deploy Models 
Players alternate placing their models, with the player 
with the most models starting ﬁrst. If the numbers of 
models are equal, players roll oﬀ with the high roller 
starting their deployment ﬁrst. The ﬁrst player to 
deploy anything can select whether to use Deploy Zone 
1, 2, 3 or 4, other players may then choose one of the 
other untaken Deploy Zones. Each player deploys a 
total of half (rounding up) of their models anywhere in 
their respective Deploy Zone. 
Special Rules 
Back-Up 
Each player’s remaining models are nearby and ready 
to provide back‐up after the rumble starts. Include the 
Action chips for all of the models in the bag. When a 
model that isn’t on the table is activated it must take a 
Move action to move on from any part of the table edge 
that is within its Deploy Zone as its ﬁrst action before 
choosing its second action. A double‐action may not be 
chosen when entering the table. 
Leaving the Table 
Models in a rumble can leave the table if they move 
across a table edge by any means. Models that leave 
the table for any reason do not return.  
Game Length 
The game continues until only one player has models 
left on the tabletop and all other models have left the 
tabletop or been incapacitated or subdued. 
Objectives 
The player left in control of the table claims victory as 
king of the rumble! 
SENTENCING 
The following sections of the Book of Law will apply in a 
Rumble. Any model being sentenced will automatically 
gain an additional D6 years for possession of contraband, 
unlicensed weaponry, overdue vid‐slugs and the like. 
• Section 1: Homicide; Section 2: Assault; Section 6: 
Arson; Section 8: Criminal Damage; Section 9: 
Illegal Imprisonment & Abduction; Section 10: 
Technology; Section 11 Public Order; Section 12: 
Public Nuisance; Section 15: Pollution; Section 16: 
Perverting the Course of Justice.

THE SET UP 
Chases and ambushes out on the streets will only get 
you so far. Sooner or later it’s time to go after the 
enemy on their home turf and seize their most valuable 
assets. Raid is best suited for medium to large sized 
games. Vehicles are permitted for the attackers in a raid 
scenario, but the only vehicle permitted for the 
defender is the get‐away van (see below). The defender 
can also invest in defences if desired. 
The caches might be alien weaponry, Suspended 
Animation units containing helpless citizens about to be 
smuggled oﬀ world into slavery, illegal stashes of 
comics, crates of BOING® dispensers, or Stookie 
Glands. Whatever the contraband is, some perp is 
paying good money to have it stolen – or the Chief 
Judge absolutely needs it oﬀ the streets. 
THE ENCOUNTER 
Terrain Placement  
The table must include open ground across one half of 
the table. The open ground can still include objects like 
walls, fences, rad pits, parked vehicles etc. but no 
buildings larger than a guard hut. The other half of the 
table should have one or more prominent buildings to 
represent the facility being raided.  
THE RAID
SCENARIO 4
GET-AWAY VAN 
The defender can opt to place a Hover‐Van on 
the table at the start of deployment. This 
vehicle occupies a space no bigger than 4" by 4" 
and should be represented by a suitable model.  
Any defending model with a cache marker can 
spend a single action in contact with the Hover‐
Van to stash its marker on‐board rather than 
having to leave the table with it. An attacking 
model can take a marker back out of this stash 
by spending a single action in contact.  
The defending player can choose for the 
Hover‐Van to lift oﬀ and escape at the end of 
any turn. The stash aboard the van is then safe. 
Deploy Models 
The defending player deploys half (rounding down) of 
their models as sentries anywhere that is not in the 
open area of the table. The attacking models move on 
from the indicated table edge when they activate on 
the ﬁrst turn.
THE RAID SCENARIO MAP
ATTACKER’S TABLE EDGE
OPEN  
GROUND
BUILDING


---

## PDF Page 137

135
Special Rules 
Caches 
At the start of deployment, the players place ﬁve cache 
markers, starting with the defending player and then 
alternating. Each marker must be at least 6" from a 
table edge and at least 6" from another cache marker. 
Any model that spends a single action in contact with a 
marker can move it. Whilst moving a cache marker, a 
model may not Sprint or Charge unless they drop the 
cache at the start of their movement. If a model with a 
cache marker is pinned, stunned, subdued or 
incapacitated the cache is dropped on the spot.  
An attacking model can move cache markers too, and 
captures any they move oﬀ the attacker’s table edge.  
A defending model can stash a cache marker by moving 
it oﬀ any other table edge (or loading it onto a get‐away 
van – see the box out).  
Sentries 
Until the alarm is sounded, when a sentry is 
activated roll a Combat dice; on a [HIT] they 
may make one normal Move action. On a 
[ARMOUR] the sentry takes no action and 
remains stationary. On a [SPECIAL], the attacker 
may make a normal Move action with the 
sentry. Star chips make no diﬀerence except 
that, as normal, they may be placed back in the 
pot with a successful Cool test (and result in 
being pinned on a failed attempt). 
If a sentry draws a line of sight to an enemy model while 
taking a Move it stops immediately and tests to spot 
them. If the test is failed, it will continue its movement 
and cannot test again to spot that model.  
During any subsequent activation, the sentry model 
may test to spot any enemy model that takes an action 
within the sentry’s Focused Front arc (FF).  
To spot enemies, roll a Combat dice. What they  
need to score depends on how much cover the 
attacking model is in.  
Heavy cover  
Light cover 
No cover  
On a successful roll the alarm is raised. 
Test only once per activation per enemy model in line  
of sight. 
A sentry will automatically detect an attacker in their 
Focused Front arc if they are within their Cool value in 
inches, regardless of line of sight. 
The alarm is also raised if any attacking model uses 
an attack action and fails to incapacitate or subdue the 
target, Charges, Sprints, or Moves with a cache marker.  
Vehicles will raise the alarm immediately if they move 
onto the tabletop. Sentries trying to spot mounted 
models roll two Combat dice and succeed if either roll is 
the required result.  
Back-Up 
The defending player’s remaining models are nearby 
and ready to reinforce after the alarm is sounded. When 
the alarm is sounded or after the ﬁrst turn is over, 
whichever comes ﬁrst, add the Action chips for all 
surviving defending models into the bag. When a model 
that isn’t on the table is activated it must take a Move 
action to move on from any part of the table edge that 
is within of its deploy zone as its ﬁrst action, before 
choosing its second action. A double‐action may not be 
chosen when entering the table. Models moving onto 
the table before the alarm is raised act as additional 
sentries until the alarm is raised. 
Leaving the Table 
Models can leave the table if they move across a table 
edge by any means. Attacking and defending models 
that leave the table for any reason can return using the 
Back‐Up rules.  
Game Length 
The game continues until all models of one faction have 
left the tabletop, been incapacitated or subdued. 
Objectives 
Securing the caches is essential and because both sides 
want them they cannot be destroyed. At the end of the 
game, count up the number of cache markers secured 
by each player. The player with the greatest number of 
cache markers is the winner. 
SENTENCING 
When it comes to sentencing, a lot will depend on 
exactly what the caches represent. If it’s not been 
decided previously (and it’s entirely possible the raid is 
occurring without that precise Intel) roll a D6 to 
determine their contents: 1‐2 equals Contraband,  
3‐4 equals Illegal Imprisonment, 5‐6 equals Medical  
& Scientific. 
• Section 1: Homicide; Section 2: Assault;  
Section 3: Robbery; Section 4: Burglary; Section 5: 
Theft & Theft‐Related Oﬀences; Section 6: Arson;  
Section 7: Medical & Scientiﬁc; Section 8:  
Criminal Damage; Section 9: Illegal Imprisonment  
& Abduction; Section 10: Technology; Section 13: 
Impersonation & Deception; Section 14: 
Contraband; Section 15: Pollution; Section 16: 
Perverting the Course of Justice.

THE SET UP 
The Heist is suited to any size of games. Vehicles are 
permitted in a Heist scenario. In a Heist the attackers 
are targeting a small building or a vehicle that contains 
the caches of goods they are after in a quick, hopefully 
overwhelming attack.  
This is a smash‐and‐grab so it’s less likely that Judges 
will be attackers in this kind of scenario, although there 
may be circumstances where this kind of approach may 
be called for, such as a hostage situation. 
THE ENCOUNTER 
Terrain Placement  
The table must include a crossroads with streets 12"‐18" 
wide running across the table from each side to the 
other. The areas in the four corner sections can have 
buildings but may include parks, parking lots, waste 
ground, rad pits, etc. The cache location is placed in the 
centre of the crossroads and as mentioned above might 
be a large (immobile) vehicle or a small building. 
Deploy Models 
The defending player deploys half of their models 
(rounding down in this case) within 6" of the centre of the 
table. The attacking player deploys all of their models 
anywhere that is more than 9" from an enemy model.  
Big Meg Cards 
A brazen heist in broad daylight is sure to bring out 
the crazies and other complications. Both players 
draw one additional Big Meg card after all models 
have been deployed. 
THE HEIST
SCENARIO 5

## PDF Page 139

Special Rules 
Caches 
At the start of deployment, the players place three 
cache markers, starting with the defending player and 
then alternating. Each marker must be within 6" of the 
centre of the table. Any model that spends one action in 
contact with a marker can move it. Whilst moving a 
cache marker, a model may not Sprint or Charge unless 
they drop the cache at the start of their movement. If a 
model with a cache marker is pinned, stunned, subdued 
or incapacitated the cache is dropped on the spot. An 
attacking model captures any cache it moves oﬀ a table 
edge. A defending model can move a cache marker if 
desired, but in this scenario has no way to safely stash it.  
Back-Up 
The defending player’s remaining models are nearby 
and ready to come to the rescue. Include the Action 
chips for all of the defending models in the bag. When a 
model that isn’t on the table is activated it must take a 
Move action to move on from any part of the table edge 
that is within of their Deploy Zone as its ﬁrst action 
before choosing its second action. A double‐action may 
not be chosen when entering the table. 
Leaving the Table 
Attacking models can leave the table if they move 
across a table edge by any means. Models that leave 
the table for any reason may not return.  
Game Length 
The game continues until all attacking models have left 
the tabletop, or have been incapacitated or subdued. 
Objectives 
The attacking side need to carry oﬀ two or more cache 
markers over a table edge so they can claim victory. The 
defending side wins if they prevent this. 
SENTENCING 
The following sections of the Book of Law may apply in 
a Heist. As with a Raid it may not be clear what the 
caches represent before making the attempt, if that’s 
the case roll a D6 to determine their contents: 
1‐2 = Contraband,  
3‐4 = Illegal Imprisonment,  
5‐6 = Medical & Scientiﬁc. 
• Section 1: Homicide; Section 2: Assault; Section 3: 
Robbery; Section 4: Burglary; Section 5: Theft & 
Theft‐Related Offences; Section 6: Arson; Section 7: 
Medical & Scientific; Section 8: Criminal Damage; 
Section 9: Illegal Imprisonment & Abduction; Section 
10: Technology; Section 13: Impersonation & 
Deception; Section 14: Contraband; Section 15: 
Pollution; Section 16: Perverting the Course of Justice.

## PDF Page 140

THE SET UP 
Mo‐pad Madness is suited to large games. Vehicles are 
permitted and at least some vehicles and/or mounted 
models are recommended.  
A sizeable proportion of Mega‐City One’s population 
inhabit eighteen million ‘Mo‐pads’ – mobile homes – in 
constant motion circling around the city’s thousands of 
miles of Megaways, overzooms and underzooms 
alongside automated pods, big‐rigs and robo‐liners. 
Crimes on the fast‐moving Megaways oﬀer singular and 
often lethal problems for perpetrators and Judges alike. 
THE ENCOUNTER 
Terrain Placement  
Creative thinking is necessary for Mo‐pad Madness.  
The table is divided into three parallel lanes, each 
approximately 12" wide. Each lane is occupied by a 
number of vehicles beween 4" and 6" apart from each 
other. Appropriate models are ideal, of course, and toys 
can supply a surprising variety of large‐scale sci‐ﬁ type 
vehicles quite cheaply. In more pragmatic terms, all kinds 
of packaging, small boxes and books can be pressed into 
service. The latter are particularly helpful as ﬂat tops on 
such ‘vehicles’ helps with non‐precarious model 
placement. Size is ﬂexible when it comes to vehicles, 
simply arrange them so that it’s possible for player 
vehicles and mounted models to slip between the lanes. 
MO-PAD MADNESs
MO-PAD MADNESS SCENARIO MAP
SCENARIO 6


---

## PDF Page 141

Deploy Models 
For this scenario, all models must be either mounted, in 
vehicles, or placed on top of the central Mo‐pad. The 
defending player deploys all of their models within 6" of 
the centre of the table. The attacking player deploys all 
of their models anywhere that is more than 9" from an 
enemy model.  
Special Rules 
Rolling Road 
The Mo‐pads are moving along at a fast pace. All player‐
controlled vehicles and mounted models roll at the end 
of each turn on the table below to represent how well 
they keep with the traﬃc ﬂow (models that are on top 
of a Mo‐pad do not roll).  
Mo‐pads do not roll on this table as their robot  
brains automatically keep them in position relative to 
each other. 
If this move (or any other move, like going out of 
control) brings a model into contact with a Mo‐Pad or 
another model, treat it as a ram (see page 31) counting 
a Mo‐pad as Fight 8.  
The Mo‐pad itself will not be damaged or suﬀer any 
eﬀects from the ram.  
Mounted models and player vehicles are strongly 
advised to keep this rule in mind when moving.  
Drift Kings 
Due to the rolling road eﬀect player vehicles can ‘drift’ 
sideways across lanes without having to turn. Deduct 1" 
of forward progress for each 1" drifted sideways. If a 
vehicle moves further sideways than forward take a 
Cool test for the driver, failure means the vehicle goes 
out of control (see page 32). 
The ﬂoor is lava! 
If a model on foot comes into contact with the table 
surface for any reason they must pass a Cool test to 
miraculously swing themselves aboard the nearest Mo‐
pad or other vehicle. If they fail the test they are… well 
let’s just say incapacitated with no chance of survival at 
the end of the game, no need for gory details, even 
Resyk won’t get anything out of this one. 
Back-Up 
There is no back‐up in this scenario. 
Leaving the Table 
Defending models can leave the table if they move 
across the ‘forward’ or ‘backward’ table edge by  
any means. Mounted models and models on foot can 
also move across the side edges of the table if they  
can get to them and pass a Cool test. There is no 
penalty for failing the test and it can be attempted 
again on subsequent activations as the model waits for 
the right opportunity. 
Models that leave the table for any reason may  
not return. 
Game Length 
The game continues until all of either the attacker’s or 
defender’s models have left the tabletop, been 
incapacitated or subdued. 
Objectives 
The defending side need to get at least half of their 
models (rounding up) oﬀ the table to claim victory, the 
attacking side wins by preventing this. 
SENTENCING 
The following sections of the Book of Law may apply in 
Mo‐pad Madness.  
• Section 1: Homicide; Section 2: Assault; Section 3: 
Robbery; Section 4: Burglary; Section 5: Theft &  
Theft‐Related Oﬀences; Section 6: Arson; Section 8: 
Criminal Damage; Section 15: Pollution; Section 16: 
Perverting the Course of Justice.

## PDF Page 142

PLAYING A CAMPAIGN 
It is entirely possibly to run an open‐ended Judge Dredd 
campaign. However, as such campaigns progress they 
tend to lose impetus, players drop out or one faction 
might develop such an advantage that it seems time to 
wrap things up. To give your campaign some focus, and 
to manage the commitment needed, the players can 
set goals at the start of the campaign to determine how 
long they want the campaign to continue. During the 
campaign the players will be able to increase the 
Notoriety of their band. A campaign ends once a faction 
reaches the required number of games. The faction 
with the highest total Notoriety wins the campaign. 
• Short campaign (around 3 games) 
• Medium campaign (around 6 games) 
• Long campaign (around 9 games) 
STARTING FACTIONS 
Unlike one ‐oﬀ games, in which players simply select 
faction members as part of their Set  Up, a campaign 
allows the players a persistent cast of characters from 
which they draw their ﬁghters for a particular game. As 
the campaign progresses, they can add other characters 
and advance existing ones to expand their options. 
140
CAMPAIGN PLAY FOR JUDGE DREDd 
The Judge Dredd campaign is an open system that allows 
players to get together, have some potentially irregular 
games, and chart the course of their stories through a 
connected narrative. The generational system means that 
players’ factions develop as and when they play, so there’s 
no need to have a formal schedule of games like in a league. 
You will ﬁnd it useful to appoint one player to oversee the 
campaign, called ‘Barney’ in honour of the Mega‐City One’s 
administrative Computer. Barney’s job is to ensure that 
players can arrange their games and help communicate 
what’s been happening in the campaign (perhaps with a 
newsletter or setting up a forum or social media group). 
There’s no reason Barney can’t run a faction in the 
campaign at the same time. 
To actually take part in the campaign all a player needs to 
do is pick a leader and starting faction, and then play 
games. During each game they note 
down sentences handed down on 
those captured, and infractions 
generated by Judges to keep track 
of their on‐going status. They have 
an opportunity to gain Notoriety 
and increase in rank, hire more 
faction members or procure 
special upgrades for their lairs or 
Sector House.


---

## PDF Page 143

The recommended size for a starting gang roster is 50 
Notoriety (‘gang’ includes Judges in this context). One 
model with a Cool 4+ should be nominated as the 
Leader. The remaining Notoriety is spent recruiting and 
equipping more characters as a supporting cast. No 
other member of the gang can exceed half the 
Notoriety value of the Leader at the start of the 
campaign. Note that in order to utilise any of the 
abilities of your Leader, they must actually be present in 
the scenario about to be played. 
NAMED CHARACTERS 
The gang may only contain one each of the named 
characters in the Denizens of Mega‐City One listings. 
It is possible for multiple players to use the same 
characters, but you cannot have two Judge Andersons 
being used by the same player, for example. 
FACTION NOTORIETY 
Each player’s faction has an overall Notoriety value, 
which is the total Notoriety of all of its characters plus 
any vehicles, specialists, base upgrades and other 
factors as discussed later. This total Notoriety is  
always used when working out if one side or the other 
is the underdog in a campaign game, regardless of 
which actual models are being used in an Encounter. 
Don’t forget to increase faction Notoriety for 
promotions, or to temporarily reduce overall Notoriety 
for destroyed vehicles. 
THE GENERATION GAME 
A Judge Dredd campaign is generational, which is to say 
it depicts years, or even decades of time passing within 
which a few key encounters get played as games. So a 
Juve might progress to become a Punk and then a Heavy, 
or even a Leader in their own right, over the course of a 
campaign. Equally, a Cadet Judge might progress up to 
Rookie and then to full Judge, and even Street Judge.  
Players are advised to keep this in mind when they 
select their starting gang – you will only be able to 
regularly use these characters in your games, so ensure 
there are plenty of opportunities for growth. 
The generation campaign is split into Early, Mid and 
Late phases. Which is why the suggested campaign 
length is divisible by three. This is not essential, but  
you will need to decide where any extra games ﬁt into 
the schedule. 
Example: 
The Early Phase 
In Early phase games, when choosing models from their 
gang roster, players are limited to playing a gang up to 
a maximum total Notoriety of 25. 
The Mid Phase 
In Mid phase games, players can choose up to 50 
Notoriety, when picking their forces for games. 
The Late Phase 
In Late phase games, choose from up  
to 75 Notoriety. Players with rosters 
that are below the requisite Notoriety 
get the diﬀerence to spend in  
each game, to make up  
any shortfall.
TO LIVE AND DIE IN

SUBDUED, INCAPACITATED, SENTENCED 
During the course of a stand‐alone game, it matters 
little if a model that has been taken down is likely to 
suﬀer any long‐term eﬀects from their injuries. 
However, in a campaign there are greater 
consequences that need to be considered. 
Most characters in the Judge Dredd comic strip only 
suﬀer crippling injuries, get captured, or die when it 
serves some greater narrative purpose. For this reason, 
these campaign rules assume that characters will 
eventually make a recovery or escape from their 
captors, and so on – characters are usually only 
temporarily removed from the faction roster. During 
campaign play, characters may only suﬀer longer‐term 
eﬀects from being subdued, incapacitated and 
sentenced as explained next. 
SUBDUED 
Make a Cool test for each model on the losing side that 
is subdued at the end of the game. On a pass, they 
somehow manage to elude capture and return to their 
companions unharmed.  
On a fail, the 
outcome depends 
on whether Judges 
are involved in the 
encounter or not. If 
Judges are present 
they are caught, 
arrested and will 
proceed to 
sentencing.  
If no Judges are 
present the model is 
hounded by the 
victors and is at ‐1 
Cool in their next 
game due to their 
exertions in escaping 
– hiding in a sewer  
UMPTY BAGgING 
Created by Uncle Ump, Umpty Candy tasted so good that even the tiniest amount 
would induce cravings and hallucinations if the addiction was not fed. The 
Judges, confronted with an epidemic of Umpty withdrawal, knew that Uncle Ump, 
who held the recipe in his head, could not be charged with a crime. Judge Dredd 
therefore had the inventor placed in a spacecraft and sent into deep space on a 
long orbit. This exile would ensure he could not reveal the formula to 
unscrupulous perps.  
Unfortunately the plan went badly wrong when the spacecraft was 
intercepted by the Jong Brothers, Chump Dumpers who forced the recipe 
from Ump before spacing him. Within months Mega-City One was flooded 
with fake Umpty Candy before the Justice department shut the Jongs 
down. Despite this the Jongs had sold-on the recipe to others who 
periodically still try to get fake Umpty Candy onto the streets.
or trash receptacle (but not trash grinder!), wading 
through a rad pit, etc. This temporary deduction  
does  aﬀect the type of chip they generate in the  
next game.  
If they do not participate in the next encounter, or if the 
campaign moves to another phase, the model regains 
their composure and the penalty is lifted in time for the 
following encounter. 
Subdued models on the winning side manage to escape 
without further incident, as do those making use of a 
Bolthole or Emergency Evac upgrade (see later).  
INCAPACITATED 
At the end of the game roll a Resist test for 
each incapacitated model, if they fail to roll a 
[ARMOUR] result, they get a ride on the meat 
wagons to Resyk to be rendered into a variety 
of useful organic compounds for the city. 
Strike these models from the roster – life is 
harsh in Mega‐City One. Judges count both 
[SPECIAL] and [ARMOUR] as a success when they 
make this roll.

## PDF Page 145

A recovering character has an Injury marker placed on 
them at the start of the game. This cannot be removed 
during the course of the game, and so in eﬀect they are 
at ‐1 to all of their characteristics.  
It is possible for a recovering character to receive 
additional wounds, but only one marker is ever used. If 
they have multiple wounds, each subsequent recovery 
time adds its duration to the time to heal. 
Full Recovery 
Normally a character heals one level of wound per 
game played. So, for example, a character with a 
Serious Wound that takes part in an encounter will 
reduce this to a Minor Wound for the following game. If 
they are wounded again, the recovery time is added on 
to the number of games before they fully recover.  
A model can spend time away from the action to 
recuperate. For each game that they miss, reduce their 
level of wound by two levels (from Acute to Minor, for 
example). Even though recuperating, their Notoriety is 
still used to calculate the faction roster’s total Notoriety. 
Going to a MediBot can also speed up the recovery 
process, as discussed in the base upgrade section later. 
This is in addition to recovery gained for missing a 
game. By default, Judges always have access to all the 
best MediBots or Speedhealing the city has to oﬀer.  
If the campaign moves from one phase to another (i.e. 
from Early to Mid, or Mid to Late) any recovering models 
automatically make a full recovery in that time, possibly 
with some bits of them being replaced by bionic implants. 
143
Any perp that survives being incapacitated gets 
arrested if Judges are present in the opposing faction, 
being put on med‐wagons and sent for sentencing. 
Surviving Judges will be back on the streets within 
twenty‐four hours. 
Incapacitated models that survive may suﬀer penalties 
in their next encounter due to their on‐going injuries. 
For each surviving incapacitated model roll a D6 and 
refer to the Injuries Table. Each Injury marker on the 
model above its starting Resist value deducts 1 from  
the roll. 
INJURIES TABLE 
For example, Street Judge Bollands is taken down 
with a total of one Stunned marker and ﬁve Injury 
markers. The Judge survived being incapacitated and 
must now roll a D6, deducting 1 from the result as he 
has a Resist of 4, and ﬁve injuries. He rolls a 5, which 
is downgraded to a 4, giving him a Minor Wound. 
Pretty lucky, all things considered.

SENTENCING 
If any Judges are present during an encounter, and 
not all them end up incapacitated or subdued at the 
end of the game, they will deliver immediate 
sentencing on any captured perps from the opposing 
faction. Total up the perp’s sentences as detailed in 
the Scenarios section. 
As a quick and dirty alternative you can simply roll a D6 
and consult the Sentencing Table for the outcome. 
Deduct three from the dice roll if the perps harmed a 
Judge, and one if they harmed citizens (including 
opposing gang members).

served their sentence, although early release might see 
them return ahead of time.

social programs make frequent attempt to rehabilitate 
perpetrators back into the community, in combination 
with PSU monitoring; or robo‐minders and buddy 
systems that all stand no chance of curbing a truly 
determined criminal. Beyond this, bureaucratic 
incompetence, or an early release for good behaviour

Mounts are considered a part of the model

## PDF Page 147

JUDGES IN THE CAMPAIGN 
Judges occupy a somewhat unique role in a campaign 
as they are the ones handing down sentences and 
have the full backing of the Justice Department. 
However, the Judges also have to concern themselves 
with day‐to‐day law enforcement and are always 
spread thin, so the following rules apply to players 
using Judges in a campaign. 
IN THE LINE OF DUTY MEANS IN THE LINE OF FIRE 
Although Judges do not have to worry about being 
imprisoned (unless they run afoul of the SJS) and 
receive the best medical care available they are always 
on the frontlines of Justice and therefore at constant 
risk of death or career‐ending injury. It’s also possible 
for a Senior Judge to be reassigned to cover a desperate 
shortage in another Sector, be sent on a special mission 
outside the city, or to reach the natural end of their 
career and decide it is time they took the Long Walk 
into the Cursed Earth or the Undercity. 
At the end of each campaign phase roll a D6 for each 
model of Street Judge rank or higher on a Justice 
Department player’s roster and add the Judge’s cool to 
the result. If the total of the roll + Cool is 10 or more 
that Judge is removed from the player’s roster. Only 
one Judge can be lost in this fashion in each campaign 
phase, if multiple Judges are due to be removed the 
Justice Department player chooses which one is lost. 
Notoriety lost from the player’s roster is replaced with 
new models, however the Justice Department player 
may only select Cadets, Rookies and/or basic Judges, or 
additional weapons, vehicles and equipment, to make 
up the shortfall.  
SJS INVESTIGATIONS 
Certain actions, like repeated leniency or the reckless 
endangerment of civilians, will bring the ever‐watchful 
eye of the SJS. Once an investigation starts, only the 
most upright and spotless Judges have a chance of 
exoneration, and sometimes a totally clean record will 
only serve to convince the SJS that a wider conspiracy is 
taking place.  
SJS investigations will be triggered by speciﬁc actions 
taken by Judges in games. The opposing player 
should pay close attention and helpfully 
remind the Justice Department when 
they are about to risk an SJS 
investigation into their actions. 
The following acts bring 
down the wrath of the 
SJS and place a 
Judge under 
investigation.
Recklessly Endangering Citizens 
Using Blast eﬀect weapons (other than Stun) with 
citizens in the area of eﬀect, or making ranged shots 
that end up hitting citizens.  
Use of vehicle-mounted weapons  
on targets that are not robots or vehicles 
It is considered an excessive use of force to use these 
weapons when a Lawgiver should be more than 
adequate. Again, stun weapons like the Pat wagon’s 
Paciﬁer are not deemed excessive.  
Note, these restrictions usually only apply within Mega‐
City One; there is no excessive force in the Cursed 
Earth, just as there are usually no citizens out there 
(although that is possible).  
Harming another Judge 
Taking an action that results in another Judge being 
injured (not stunned). 
The complexities of reviewing a Judge’s past record, 
mitigating circumstances, evidence gathering 
and ﬁnal trial are, essentially, arbitrary in 
outcome. Make a Cool test for every Judge 
under SJS investigation before the next 
encounter and total the number of [ARMOUR] 
and [SPECIAL] results they roll. 
INVESTIGATION TABLE 
As for In the Line of Duty (above), any Notoriety lost 
from the player’s roster due to an SJS investigation is 
replaced with new models. The Justice Department 
player may only select Cadets, Rookies and/or basic 
Judges, or additional weapons, vehicles and 
equipment, to make up the shortfall.

## PDF Page 148

DEeDS, PROMOTIONS & INCREASING NOTORIETY  
In a campaign, models in your faction can have 
performed Deeds that give them a chance to be 
promoted and increase their individual Notoriety. 
Additionally, each faction will gain more Notoriety to 
spend on additional models and equipment, like 
vehicles or base upgrades.  
DEeDS 
Taking part in games and performing particular tasks 
during Encounters earns a character Deeds. As a 
character gains Deeds, they get the chance to be 
promoted to the next rank in their respective faction.  
EARNING DEeDS 
A model gets a Deed for the following achievements, 
regardless of their faction: 
• Incapacitating or subduing an opposing model of 
equal or higher Notoriety earns a Deed – only the 
model that inﬂicts the Stun or Injury marker that 
takes the foe down gets the Deed. 
• Destroying a vehicle or mounted model with a 
single attack or shot earns a Deed. 
• Using a Big Meg or Armoury  
card marked with a Deeds symbol 
(see right). 
• One model, nominated by the 
winning player, earns a Deed at the end 
of the game.  
• Every model on the player roster after the ﬁnal 
encounter of a campaign phase earns a Deed.
Deeds are also earned depending upon the scenario as 
listed below: 
• Models that escape from the table in a Foot Chase 
scenario earn a Deed. 
• Models that survive being ambushed in an 
Ambush scenario earn a Deed. 
• Models that survive to the end of a Rumble 
scenario earn a Deed. 
• Models that capture or stash a cache marker in a 
Raid scenario earn a Deed. 
• Models that capture a cache marker in a Heist 
scenario earn a Deed. 
• Models that escape from the table in a Mo‐pad 
Madness scenario earn a Deed. 
Note that to ‘survive’ in this context means not 
getting subdued or incapacitated during the scenario 
Additionally, models on a player’s roster that do not 
participate in a scenario cannot claim to have 
survived it. 
Judges also gain Deeds as listed below. The Justice 
Department player assigns any Deeds earned from 
sentencing to the attending Judges as they see ﬁt. 
• Sentencing a perp to 20 years or more earns the 
Judge a Deed. 
Criminals gain additional Deeds as listed below: 
• Being sentenced to 10 years or more in the cubes 
earns the model a Deed for each ten years or part 
thereof of its sentence.
146
BIG SMELlY 
By the early years of the 21st century the eastern seaboard of the 
United States had become heavily polluted and almost uninhabitable. 
Rather than clean this mess up, the government decided to build  
Mega-City One on top of it. The old rancid environment is generally 
known as the undercity and is the home of mutants, troggies, 
werewolves, and many other unspeakable horrors. Perhaps the most 
toxic feature of the undercity is the once great Ohio river, now so 
polluted it has taken the name of the Big Smelly. All sensible 
citizens avoid going down to this subterranean hell but occasionally 
Justice Department hunter-Killer teams are sent down to cull 
mutant and troggie numbers, whilst a number of Judges taking the 
Long Walk have elected to make this their final destination.

## PDF Page 149

PROMOTIONS 
Deeds allow a model to be promoted in rank. Promotion ranks for the Judges, Block Gang and Citi‐Def factions are: 
If a model has one or more Deeds at the end of 
a game make a Cool test for it, add one extra 
dice for each Deed the model achieved above 
one. [SPECIAL] and [HIT] results count as 
successes for this Cool test. If the number of 
successes rolled is equal to or higher than the 
next rank number the model is promoted the next rank.  
For example, a Rookie Judge (rank 2) performs two  
Deeds during a game, so they test for promotion by  
rolling 3 dice in total; 2 for the Rookie’s Cool plus one  
extra for performing two Deeds. If the Rookie rolls 
three or more [SPECIAL] and/or [HIT] results on the 
combined, dice they will be promoted to a full Judge 
(Rank 3). About time too, Rookie. 
When a model is promoted it gains the stat line and 
skills of its new rank, and it loses any skills that no longer 
apply from the old rank, as well as any Deeds it has 
earned so far. A model can only be promoted one rank at 
a time. If a model is recovering from a wound or serving 
cube time when it is promoted it still has to suﬀer the 
consequences for the requisite number of games.  
If the promotion roll is failed, no promotion occurs, but 
keep a note of the model’s number of Deeds. The model 
can test again at the end of the next game it 
participates in, potentially with even more Deeds. 
Testing for promotion is always voluntary, a player 
doesn’t have to test for promotion for some or all of 
their models if they don’t want to. 
Judge Specialisations 
When a Rookie or Judge gains a promotion, they can 
elect to become a specialist Psi, Tek or Med Judge 
instead of increasing their rank. Being a specialist will 
limit them to being promoted to the rank of Judge in 
the case of Teks and Meds, or Street Judge in the case 
of Psis, in the future. Note that a model can only ever 
select a single specialisation.

BASE UPGRADES 
When selecting a faction roster, Notoriety can be spent 
on base upgrades. Upgrades marked with * may be 
purchased more than once. 
DEFENCES . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . Cost: Variable* 
You may use Notoriety to purchase Home Base 
Defences and have them available whenever they 
must defend against a raid. See opposite for  
specific defences. 
INFORMERS (NARKS) . . . Cost: 3 Notoriety* 
Players may spend Notoriety to employ Informers. 
When determining which side takes which role in a 
scenario (i.e. attacker or defender), before rolling the 
dice, a faction may expend an Informer to add one to 
the roll. They may expend more than one Informer per 
encounter, gaining +1 for each. Once used, the Informer 
is removed from the faction’s resources until the next 
phase of the campaign. Factions secretly write down 
how many Informers they wish to use and reveal their 
intentions together. 
BOLTHOLE OR  
EMERGENCY EVAC . . . . . Cost: 5 Notoriety* 
Each Bolthole or Emergency Evac allows one subdued 
model to automatically escape pursuit without a Cool 
test. See the subdued rules for details. 
MEDIBOT/ 
SPEEDHEAL COUCHES . . Cost: 5 Notoriety* 
Each MediBot or Speedheal couch allows one 
incapacitated model to downgrade the level of their 
wound by one, or, if they pass a Resist test, by two . See 
the incapacitated rules for details. 
TURF . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . Cost: 10 Notoriety* 
Gangs only. A Gang leader can expand their Turf, 
bringing in more income through racketeering, 
gambling and so forth. Each Turf generates D6 
additional Notoriety for the faction roster that can be 
used before each game. The Notoriety increase is 
temporary and is lost at the end of the game. 
WEAPONS LOCKER . . . . . Cost: 10 Notoriety 
A faction with a weapon locker gains 3D6 additional 
Notoriety to use for purchasing additional weapons 
during a single scenario in each phase of the campaign. 
Once used, the weapons locker is removed from the 
faction’s resources until the next phase of the 
campaign. These weapons are one‐oﬀ purchases and 
are lost at the end of the game. 
WORKSHOP. . . . . . . . . . . Cost: 10 Notoriety* 
After each game, each Workshop can be used to repair 
a single robot. A Repair allows an incapacitated or 
subdued robot to ignore the eﬀects and participate in 
the next encounter without penalty.  
ARMOURY CACHE . . . . . . Cost: 15 Notoriety 
During the Draw Armoury Cards stage of Set Up draw an 
extra Armoury card, but keep the same number as normal. 
GARAGE . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . Cost: 15 Notoriety 
A faction with a garage does not have to wait for  
the end of the current campaign phase to replace 
destroyed vehicles. 
ARSENAL . . . . . . . . . . . . . . Cost: 30 Notoriety 
Requires Armoury Cache. During the Draw Armoury 
Cards stage of Set Up draw an extra Armoury card, but 
keep the same number as normal.

known, but before any models are placed. They must 
be placed where your faction models can deploy.

PLACED GUNS. . . . . . . . . . . . Cost: Variable Notoriety 
These weapons are sited to cover approaches to 
the gang’s territory, or perhaps they have invested 
in tripods that allow them to quickly set up a 
defensive position.  
A suitable looking model should be used to 
represent a Placed Gun. Any long gun or heavy 
weapon can be turned into a placed gun by paying 
extra as shown on the table below. Placed Guns 
cannot be moved from their deployment position.

for instance). Placed Guns add 1 to the ﬁrer’s shoot 
value and grant cover to any model in contact. 
A placed gun can be sited on a Watch Tower. 
AUTO-TURrET . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . Cost: 3 Notoriety 
Possessing a small artiﬁcial brain, an auto‐turret is 
capable of discerning friend from foe – usually – and 
opening ﬁre when required.  
An auto‐turret is an upgrade to a Placed Gun.  
Example: The Heavy Spit Gun above could be turned 
into an auto‐turret for a total cost of 12 Notoriety 
The gun does not require a model to be crewed, and 
at the start of each turn will go into Overwatch. It 
has Shoot 2 (but does not count the normal +1 for 
Placed Guns). It will ﬁre at the ﬁrst enemy model 
that enters or moves across its line of sight during a 
turn. If a friendly model moves within the auto‐
turret’s line of sight, roll a D6. On a roll of a 1 it 
malfunctions and opens ﬁre anyway. Each time an 
auto‐turret opens ﬁre roll a Combat dice.  
On a [HIT] place it back into Overwatch. It 
cannot ﬁre again during the same action. 
The auto‐turret can be targeted and counts as a 
model with Resist 2 and Cool 2 for injuries and 
pinning. It cannot be stunned, but if it takes any 
injury it is destroyed. 
An auto‐turret opening ﬁre will raise the alarm in Raid. 
WATCH TOWER. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . Cost: 5 Notoriety 
This defensive position might well be a purpose‐
built construction overlooking the lair or base, or 
else simply a convenient high location from which to 
spy incoming attackers. 
A Watch Tower must be crewed by a model during 
deployment. This model may ignore any special 
movement rules (for example as a sentry in Raid) 
and remains in place until normal activation begins. 
The model cannot be pinned and beneﬁts from 
heavy cover. 
In addition, a model deployed in a Watch Tower 
starts the ﬁrst turn in Overwatch. 
Example: You can put Placed Guns and Auto‐
turrets on a Watch Tower by simply paying the 
total Notoriety cost. If our Auto‐turret Heavy Spit 
Gun from earlier was on a Watch Tower the ﬁnal 
cost would be 17 Notoriety.  
TRAPS . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . Cost: 5 Notoriety 
A well placed mine or tripwire can really punish a 
hasty enemy.  
Roll a Combat dice for any enemy model 
that Charges, Sprints or Moves through 
Diﬃcult terrain. On a roll of a [SPECIAL] the 
model has activated a trap after moving. 
They are hit by the equivalent of a Power 3 
shot (and may dodge the hit, 
etc.) but may not claim 
cover bonuses to 
their Resist. If they 
are not stunned or 
injured they may 
complete their 
action, otherwise 
their activation  
ends immediately.  
A trap triggering will 
raise the alarm in a 
Raid scenario.
To live and die in mega-city one

## PDF Page 152

GRUDGE POINTS 
Factions that have a Gang Leader or Citi‐Def Squad 
Leader as their faction leader can earn Grudge points 
against other factions, that are also led by a Gang 
Leader or Citi‐Def Squad Leader, to show the growing 
enmity or obsession between the two factions. Leaders 
gain a Grudge point for each Deed earned by the other 
side during a game.  
A Leader also gains a Grudge point: 
• For each model in his faction that suﬀers a Serious 
or Acute Wound. 
• For each character in the gang that suﬀers a loss 
of Cool due to a failed roll after being subdued. 
• For being the defender in an Ambush, Raid, or 
Heist Scenario. 
Judges never earn Grudge points.
USING GRUDGE POINTS 
Grudge points come into play when the two factions 
next meet. Compare the number of Grudge points 
between the Leaders from their last game and look up 
the total of the Leader with the most Grudges below. 
These rules are not cumulative.

## PDF Page 153

151
NARrATIVE SCENARIOS 
It’s impossible to condense forty Thrill‐power ﬁlled years 
of Judge Dredd comic strips into a straightforward 
scenario and campaign system. The system included 
here has been designed to allow players to get together 
and play a game with the minimum amount of fuss and 
pre‐ arrangement – perfect for stores and clubs, where 
most players are to be found. 
However, there is also ample scope to use these rules to 
tell an even more detailed story with your miniatures, 
delving into the narratives between your games in 
discussion with the other players. The following are ideas 
to use instead of, or as well as, the system above. 
However, it requires a bit more organisation on the part 
of the players.  
The most obvious room for expansion is in the 
subdued and sentencing rules. Rather than testing to 
see if a subdued character eludes capture with a Cool 
test, you could play out a variant of the Foot Chase 
Scenario, with the escaping model trying to reach 
safety with the help of friendly reinforcements. 
Models sent to the cubes might be broken out using a 
variant of the Raid or Heist scenarios, with cache 
markers replaced by shackled prisoners. 
For Judges, the SJS may sometimes be an opponent, 
overzealously hunting down a ‘rogue’ Judge before  
an investigation is complete. Likewise, losing a  
Judge to an SJS investigation or taking the Long Walk 
can spin off into a number of narrative scenarios in its 
own right. 
Perhaps most satisfying, but requiring the most  
work, is the purely narrative campaign. Rather than 
pre‐determining the consequences of each ﬁght, the 
players, with the help of the group’s Barney perhaps, 
decide the best way to continue the story, depending 
on the result of each game. They might assign 
particular Armoury cards, choose which models are 
available, and maybe allocate speciﬁc Big Meg cards, 
all depending on the characters involved. You could 
even try to re‐enact one of the great Judge Dredd 
storylines, basing the scenario and forces on the 
events in the strip. 
And of course, you can always make up your own 
scenarios based on what’s here or in the strip. The chip 
activation system works well with more than two 
players. It’s quite possible to add a twist on the existing 
scenarios just by including a third faction. In a 
campaign, you might like to ﬁnish oﬀ each phase with a 
multi‐player game. 
Finally, look out for opportunities to expand your 
games by adding new models, scenery and cards to 
your collections with future Judge Dredd releases from 
Warlord Games – including expansion packs focussing 
on your favourite Judge Dredd storylines.
To live and die in mega-city one
BLOBS 
The brainchild of ‘Cyclops’ Pete Runcie, the Blob look was a 
body craze that surgically changed people’s faces so that they 
became virtually featureless, the so-called Blob look!. Whilst 
ostensibly a fashion statement, Runcie’s real motive was to 
create perps who could not be tracked by the PSU’s facial 
recognition network or be described by witnesses. Whilst 
initially effective the ‘Jays’ eventually cracked down on this by 
demanding that all Blobs be tattooed with an indelible ID 
number on their forehead. 


---

## PDF Page 154

APpENDIX: WEAPON VARIANTS 
Whilst a wide and varied array of weapons is presented 
in the Armoury chapter for your characters to use, an 
avid reader of Judge Dredd will know that they are only 
just a small sample. The mean streets of Mega‐City 
One are home to a vast array of weird, wonderful, and 
above all deadly weapons.  
Presented here are rules to allow you to modify some 
of the existing basic weapons in order to create some 
unique weapon variants of your own.  
USING VARIANT WEAPONS 
There are two ways of using weapon variants in your 
games. First, weapons variants can be built and given 
to your models as alternate weapons when choosing 
your faction. Secondly, you can incorporate weapon 
variants in your campaigns as perks available when 
characters advance. 
STANDARD SCENARIOS 
If both players agree, it is perfectly reasonable when 
playing a standard scenario, or a narrative scenario of 
your own design, to use weapon variants as you see ﬁt. 
However, we suggest that rather than simply equipping 
all your punks with scope‐mounted rapid ﬁring laser 
carbines, you restrict variant weapons to only your 
faction leaders. These important models will have likely 
taken all the best swag for themselves anyway, 
becoming unique personalities in your games. 
Some examples are: 
Sultans of Bling 
Gangs love to customise their personal weapons  
and equipment, making them as ﬂamboyant (and  
loud) as possible. A Gang Lieutenant may select up to 
one additional variant weapon for the requisite 
Notoriety cost. 
Experimental Weapon 
Citi‐Def squads often have access to experimental 
variant weapons supplied by legitimate arms 
manufacturers. A Citi‐Def Squad Leader may select up 
to one additional variant weapon for the requisite 
Notoriety cost. 
Cursed Earth Workshops 
Sky Raiders scavenge the Cursed Earth for all the parts 
they can ﬁnd, fusing them into uniquely improvised, yet 
entirely serviceable, weapons. A Sky Raider Pack 
Leader may select up to one additional variant weapon 
for the requisite Notoriety cost. 
CAMPAIGNS 
Alternatively, you can introduce weapon variants as an 
additional element in your campaigns. If, during the 
course of the campaign, a model gets promoted to level 
3 then it can be equipped with a weapon with single 
variant choice. If a model is promoted to level 4 then it 
can be equipped with a weapon with up to two variants. 
Of course, in both cases you add the additional 
Notoriety to the cost of the model.  
Note, in a campaign, Justice Department models can 
never be equipped with weapon variants. They have 
enough excellent weaponry to call upon already. Also, 
the additional expense such extravagances would incur 
would be looked upon very dimly by the Justice 
Department’s accounts division.  
THE VARIANT WORKBENCH 
To build your own variant weapons, start with one of 
the base weapon proﬁles listed below and add to it 
whichever upgrades you wish. Then apply any modiﬁers 
or special rules to the base weapon proﬁle in order to 
get the ﬁnal weapon proﬁle. Each base weapon and 
upgrade has a Notoriety value associated with it, total 
this up to get the ﬁnal Notoriety value for that weapon 
and add it to the Notoriety of the model using it. 
Finally, give your new weapon a suitably intimidating 
name before heading out on to the streets to wreak 
havoc with it.

## PDF Page 164

Judge Dredd is a skirmish tabletop miniatures 
battle game for two or more players set in 
the Judge Dredd universe created by the 
legendary comic creators John Wagner  
and Carlos Ezquerra. In it Street Judges 
patrol the towering City Blocks and 
crammed streets of the vast megalopolis 
Mega‐City One, a city full of criminals, 
gangs, crazies and murderers. Only  
the uncompromising resolve of the Judges 
stands in the way of total lawlessness. 
In Judge Dredd, players take control of a 
small team of Judges or Perps as they ﬁght 
for justice or survival in the mean streets 
of Mega‐City One and beyond.  
This book contains all the rules you  
need to play Judge Dredd including 
scenarios, weapon rules and proﬁle lists 
to create your own patrol of Judges and 
criminal gangs for them to sentence.
